#6803. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6798. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

#6796. The “Facial in a Bottle” Products Experts Say Actually Work

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your trick to recreating a salon glow at home.

First came “botox in a bottle.” Now, “facial in a bottle” is the latest catchy phrase permeating beauty aisles. Also sometimes labeled with the term “flash facial,” these “multi-functional formulas are designed to mimic the actual steps of a professional facial—think exfoliation, hydration, and brightening—all in one,” explains dermatologist Dr. Jessie Cheung. “The best ones do this by combining acids, enzymes, antioxidants, and hydrators to address multiple skin concerns while minimizing irritation.”

And while they’re not a replacement for in-office treatments and an esthetician’s skilled hand, they can “deliver instant or dramatic results, typically imparting a glow or resurfacing effect,” says New York-based facialist Samantha Susca. She adds that these multipurpose formulas can nicely complement salon visits and help extend results between appointments.

Ahead, we tapped our experts and fellow dermatologist Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike for their top “facial in a bottle” recommendations, and included a few picks our beauty editors use religiously. Read on for the products they say truly live up to the hype, including ones Susca uses with her own clients. Plus, expert tips for choosing the right formula for your skin type.

The path to glassier skin often starts with a good cleanser. Susca loves this option for “a gentle daily resurfacing,” noting that it helps clear congestion and remove dead skin while also hydrating, de-puffing, and reducing the look of hyperpigmentation. “Two enzymes gently exfoliate to dissolve both oil and surface keratin, while the sugar-bound vitamin C molecule (ascorbyl glucoside) is one of the most intelligent deliveries of vitamin C that brightens the skin,” she explains. “It develops into a glow-inducing, active vitamin C once the cleanser is rinsed off.” Meanwhile, ectoin, niacinamide, and caffeine help soothe the skin and reduce inflammation.

According to the brand, the water-activated, powder-to-liquid formula is best used once daily. Those with sensitive skin should start by using it for 30 seconds every other day and watch how their skin reacts before potentially increasing the frequency.

Key ingredients: Alpha hydroxy acid complex, vitamin C complex, niacinamide, ectoin, caffeine

Over-the-counter chemical peels can offer some of the powerful skin-evening effects of a professional peel. And because they’re less intense, they can be a great option for those seeking a milder treatment. “The best at-home formulas balance exfoliation with barrier support, keeping your skin brighter, smoother, and more even without irritation,” Dr. Cheung advises. She especially likes this option from Kiehl’s, which she says “combines salicylic acid, glycolic acid, and lactic acid with soothing copper gluconate for a treatment that can be used daily.”

Key Ingredients: salicylic acid, glycolic acid, lactic acid, copper gluconate

A favorite among dermatologists, estheticians, A-listers, and beauty editors, Biologique Recherche’s Lotion P50 is iconic for good reason. “This multi-acid product exfoliates while improving skin function, for immediate glow and long-term improvements,” says Dr. Cheung. The milky toner combines AHAs, BHAs, and vinegar with gluconolactone, a gentler exfoliant, and niacinamide. Together, these ingredients help remove dead skin cells, support cell turnover, regulate sebum production, and balance the skin’s pH. As the most active of the five formulas in the P50 line, it’s best suited for those with thicker or keratinized skin who can handle a stronger exfoliant. Those with thinner or more sensitive skin may prefer P50W, formulated for sensitive, oily skin, or the newer P50T.

In her review of Lotion P50 PIGM 400, formulated to target acne scars and dark marks, contributing fashion and luxury editor Moriah Polk wrote: “About a month into using P50 consistently, I walked into work, and a fellow editor stopped me mid-sentence to say my skin was glowing. And no, I wasn’t wearing makeup. What this product does best is deliver that elusive, youthful luminosity that doesn’t always photograph well but looks incredible IRL. My skin just looks fresher, brighter, and more awake—no small feat in winter, when it’s usually flaky, dull, and generally uncooperative. P50 PIGM 400 is the only thing I’ve changed in my routine, and since starting it, I haven’t had a single breakout.”

Key ingredients: gluconolactone, tartaric acid, citric acid, lactic acid, malic acid, salicylic acid, acetic acid, niacinamide

“This waxless, weightless balm-to-oil has quickly become a desert island product of mine, and a standard finishing touch in my treatments,” says Susca. “The 86 antioxidants leave the most incredible glow that mimics a 90-minute facial.” Using a blend of fruit extracts, seed oils, and emollient-rich murumuru butter, it works to protect and deeply nourish the skin while keeping it calm and balanced.

Bonus: You can also use this to prime the skin for a gua sha session.

Key ingredients: Murumuru butter, elderberry fruit extract, blueberry seed oil, green tea leaf extract

“At just $30, these peeling pads take a remarkably comprehensive approach,” says Dr. Ugwu-Dike. “The first step includes five different exfoliating acids: glycolic, lactic, mandelic, phytic acid, and gluconolactone to help remove dead skin cells and improve texture. The second step introduces retinol to smooth fine lines and ectoin to help soothe and hydrate. Rather than relying on exfoliation alone, it combines immediate surface refinement with an ingredient that has established long-term benefits for photoaging.”

Key ingredients: Glycolic, lactic, mandelic, phytic acid, and gluconolactone, retinol, ectoin

Arguably one of the best parts of a facial is leaving with a bouncier, more lifted complexion. Susca calls Ourself’s serum a standout pick thanks to its “deep hydration delivery system,” which she says “provides a deeper lifting and plumping effect while giving the skin a lit-from-within glow.” This system combines dual-weight hyaluronic acid with a booster intended to encourage the skin’s natural production of the ingredient, and an “HA protector” designed to help it stay in the skin for longer. The formula also contains bioactive peptides, said to stimulate collagen and elastin production, and brightening vitamin C and niacinamide.

Additionally, its patented delivery technology targets specific areas of the skin for the most visible impact. In two-week, company-sponsored clinical trials, “this serum has been proven to volumize areas that are losing depth, smooth fine lines, and improve tone with consistent use,” Susca notes.

Key ingredients: Bioactive peptides, HA+ complex, vitamin C, niacinamide, patented tiered-release vesicle technology

For a quick, facial-like reset, ELLE beauty commerce editor Nerisha Penrose swears by this multitasking Korean toner mist, formulated to nourish and calm skin, minimize the appearance of pores, and even out tone.

“I stay away from most buzzy skin care because I’m on a derm-prescribed plan to help erase acne breakouts and post-acne scarring. On the nights when I’m not using my retinol or topical antibiotics, I’m generously spritzing my skin with this deeply hydrating mist,” says Penrose. “The gentle formula combines soothing botanicals and antioxidants that deliver moisture, relief, and barrier strength over time. By the time I wake up, I have the most supple, softest, radiant skin.”

Key ingredients: Rose water, lactobacillus ferment, betaine, melin seed extract, ivy seed extract

“This serum is a fantastic example of exfoliation and hydration working beautifully together, and is one of the best options for dry, mature skin,” says Dr. Ugwu-Dike. “It contains 10 percent lactic acid, which helps loosen the bond between dead skin cells to improve surface texture. But what really makes this formula impactful is the inclusion of ceramides, hyaluronic acid, and glycerin, which help support moisture retention.”

Key ingredients: Lactic acid, ceramides, hyaluronic acid, glycerin

“You want a balanced mix of actives and barrier support,” says Dr. Cheung. “For exfoliation, look for AHAs (lactic, glycolic), BHAs (salicylic acid), or enzymes. For brightening, look for vitamin C, niacinamide, or tranexamic acid. For hydration and barrier support, you want hyaluronic acid, glycerin, and ceramides.”

“If you’re oily or prone to acne, go for BHAs or a combination of exfoliants,” she continues. “If you’re a dry or sensitive type, go for lactic acid or enzymes with more hydration.”

Dr. Ugwu-Dike adds: “Alpha hydroxy acids are some of my favorite ingredients when it comes to achieving that immediate glow. Glycolic acid has a small molecular size, which makes it really effective for treating photo damage and uneven texture. Lactic acid offers both exfoliating and humectant properties, which makes it excellent for somebody whose skin is also dehydrated.”

All our experts agree that it’s best to look for options with multiple active ingredients at lower, “more tolerable” percentages, especially if you have reactive skin. “High-acid concentrations can cause inflammation, which can trigger post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation that can lead to dark marks and skin scarring,” warns Dr. Ugwu-Dike.

Resist the urge to overdo it, Dr. Cheung advises. “Focus on using these with purpose, not just piling on more products. Apply after cleansing, starting once or twice a week, then increasing as tolerated. Don’t stack multiple strong actives the same night to avoid irritation.”

“Hydration is the important finishing touch,” adds Dr. Ugwu-Dike. “I love pairing an exfoliating treatment with a rich, non-irritating moisturizer because it helps create that smooth, glass-like finish people associate with facials.”

As with any skin care product, she notes, the best results come with consistency.

Dr. Jessie Cheung is a board-certified dermatologist with practices in Chicago, Illinois, and New York City.

Samantha Susca is an esthetician and founder of Refinery4 in New York City.

Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike is a NYC-based board-certified dermatologist.

#6794. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

#6789. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

#6788. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6787. Medallion Belts Are Everywhere

Consider it a chicer take on the old Western belt.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If the runways are any indication, we’ve all been loving a good, impactful belt. Marc Jacobs loaded models up with 2008-coded chain belts at his most recent show, while McQueen wrapped black leather creations around the waist, which also snaked up to the neck like collar harnesses. Celine presented hulking supersize belts that cemented creative director Michael Rider’s dedication to standout accessorizing for spring/summer 2027. Yet at the center of it all lives the medallion belt. Consider it the most wearable version of the statement belt trend.

The look is an easy way to accessorize instantly. Medallion belts act as a kind of jewelry in this case, with their chunky hardware leading the way. Whether paired with jeans and a T-shirt or slung over a little black dress, they’re both practical and slightly whimsical. Both Dior and Celine have put an emphasis on the medallion silhouette, with chic, polished medal belt buckles engraved with designer logos.

Gilded in gold tones and attached to luscious leather, the designer medallions act as modern cartouches, proudly displaying the brand’s name like a badge. It’s definitely a modern take on logomania. Dior has also used the same medallion design seen on its belts in its beauty products, proving there’s always a desire for cute, shiny things. If the shape feels inherently Marie Antoinette-ified, it’s intentional. Its inspiration draws from the 18th-century Rococo aesthetic of Dior’s couture salons.

Chloé too has released a medallion belt. While it’s less logo-driven, it has all the major hallmarks that make the look both elevated and impactful. Four golden, gleaming horse heads surround a talismanic design. The horse motif is a clear nod to the bohemian style that is closely tied to the fashion house’s history. It also heavily relates to the lineage of the medallion belt itself, which has been around for centuries (from ancient Rome’s military uniforms and beyond) but thrived during the 1970s when Western wear reached a peak obsession. The rounded shapes of modern medallion belts of today also reference the original Native American concho belts, which originated in the American Southwest during the late 1860s and blended Spanish-Mexican aesthetics.

What sets the medallion belt apart from some of the other trending belts of the moment is its inherent ease and practicality. Some brands are even taking things a step further. For Givenchy’s spring/summer 2027 show, creative director Sarah Burton gave the medallion belt a makeover by turning the gilded emblem into a usable compact mirror. These days, the medallion belt is a real rarity in fashion: it’s an opulent staple that’s actually practical, too.

#6784. The Fringe Pieces Fashion Insiders Are Wearing This Fall

Runways say this is the adornment of the season.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fringe never really goes out of style. No matter the season, you can always expect to see the embellishment swaying from the arm of a suede jacket or dangling off a leather bag. So when it comes to trends around the design detail, it’s a mere matter of what form the fringe takes. Lately, fashion’s found itself in a fringe fixation, prompting the trim to move beyond the Western-inspired looks it’s best known for.

Fringe re-emerged on the scene last fall when spring/summer 2026 catwalks from Bottega Veneta, Givenchy, and Chanel showcased the adornment as playful ornamentation for skirts, bags, and even shoes. By the fall/winter 2026 season, others were beginning to chime in with their own takes. There were the boho-inspired bags from Etro, the romantic wispy dresses from Ulla Johnson, and restrained silhouettes from Altuzarra.

Whether you’re looking for a bold statement or a little wink of style, fringe remains a simple way to add flair to any outfit. And right now, there’s a fringed version of just about any wardrobe piece you could think of. Between the classic leather staples and newer avant-garde styles, you’ll find something for every style sensibility.

Do an online search for fringe jackets, and you’ll find no shortage of options to shop. Leather and suede remain tried-and-true materials for this style of outerwear—but now there’s also a plethora of other textiles to shop as well. Opt for Toteme’s viral scarf coat if you’re in the market for a new winter coat, or this tweed number from Etro if you’re looking for a polished piece you can wear to the office.

Nili Lotan Eloi Fringe-Trimmed Suede Jacket

Simon Miller Mabel Vegan Leather Fringe Jacket

If recent fall/winter 2026 runways from Givenchy and Proenza Schouler show us anything, it’s that fringe dresses are reaching new heights. The trim is an especially pretty touch to formalwear and event dresses, providing playful movement with every step to even the simplest silhouettes.

Cara Cara Troy Floral-Print Fringe-Trimmed Cotton-Blend Jersey Midi Dress

Ciebon Nella Asymmetric Fringe Midi Dress

Cult Gaia Noosha Fringed Silk Halterneck Maxi Dress

Just like dresses, fringe skirts are experiencing a similarly sophisticated evolution. Lately, the style has favored sleek satin fringe to create a distinct piece that will immediately elevate anything it’s paired with.

Bottega Veneta Fringed Knitted Silk Midi Skirt

Alaïa Tassle Fringed Asymmetric Wool-Gabardine Midi Skirt

Anna Sui Asymmetric Floral-Flock Fringe-Trimmed Crepe Midi Skirt

The sky’s the limit when it comes to fringe bags these days. There’s long fringe and short fringe. There’s standard leather and suede trim that adds a timeless flair, or more dramatic confections made of sequins, beads, or feathers. With beaded tassel bags on the rise, it makes sense that fringe would take similar cues for an eclectic flourish.

Fendi Croissant Fringed Calf Hair Shoulder Bag

Nili Lotan Asher Fringed-Trimmed Suede Shoulder Bag

After Givenchy debuted a pair of bold fringed mules as part of its spring/summer 2026 collection, fringe exploded onto shoes—heels in particular—in a big way. These Boudoir mules, as they came to be known, introduced a playful take on fringe footwear. Now it’s an ultra-luxe, fashion-forward style that’s perfect for those who never shy away from making a statement.

#6783. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6782. A Jewel-Toned Chicago Home Inspired by Pakistani Culture

Kate Haynes of Noble Studio brought bold personality to her client’s family home.

A room painted in rich eggplant isn’t exactly a subtle introduction. But in this Wilmette, Illinois, home, that’s precisely the point. Just beyond the foyer, a dining room drenched in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal, complete with a glossy lacquered ceiling, offers the first sign that the designer at the wheel had little interest in playing it safe. And, as Kate Haynes of Noble Studio quickly discovered, neither did her client.

The pair connected over Instagram as construction on the family’s 6,500-square-foot home neared completion. With the architectural details and hard finishes already in place, Haynes was tasked with giving the new build some personality. Luckily, the homeowner, a former graphic designer, had plenty to spare. “We talked about incorporating deeply saturated colors along with a lot of personal touches,” recalls Haynes. “She is one of the warmest, most delightful people, and her home totally reflects her personality.”

What Haynes created for the family is a masterclass in exuberance, informed in part by the homeowner’s Pakistani heritage. Rather than lean into overt cultural references, Haynes borrowed from the richness of Mughal architecture and the bold, jewel-toned palettes of South Asia, weaving both together throughout the five-bedroom home. In the daughter’s bedroom, a scalloped headboard recalls a multifoil arch; elsewhere, vintage rugs lend the new construction a sense of history. “We wanted to have that original depth that can only come from a textile that has seen some life and years,” Haynes explains.

There’s plenty of levity, too: a hanging chair in the blue reading room, a rock wall in the playroom, and a custom circular banquette that rivals the best restaurant in town. Each detail speaks to a family that takes its pleasures seriously, whether that’s gathering with friends for dinner or curling up with a book. “She was just a person who looks for things to be delighted by,” Haynes says of her client. Consider the house a reflection of that philosophy.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Noble StudioBuilder: Sterling Wilson DevelopmentLocation: Wilmette, ILThe Space: A five-bedroom, seven-bathroom home across 6,500 square feet

An expansive crane mural sets a serene, transportive mood in the primary suite, where “subtle eastern influence and massive graphic appeal” establish an earthy palette, says Haynes. The blush pink upholstered bed is flanked by khaki green Lawson-Fenning nightstands, while a rustic vintage Indian teak bench adds a timeworn counterpoint to the room'så more refined furnishings.

Cornflower blue wainscoting and patterned Penny Morrison wallpaper establish a cheerful backdrop in the daughter’s bedroom, where saffron yellow accents tie the space into the rest of the home’s palette. “The homeowner had seen an inspirational image with geometric wainscoting topped with a patterned wallpaper, and it really resonated with her, so she asked us to run with that idea,” adds Haynes.

“We wanted to create serene escapes in each of the two guest rooms that we worked on,” explains Haynes of this enveloping escape, swathed in a taupey pink. “We loved the Penny Morrison block printed graphic fabric that is on the drapes—the coppery brown is so rich against the quieter wall.”

“Our client is an avid reader, and she had wanted to create a dreamy blue space with a hanging chair where she can eventually cultivate plants that will become a living, breathing part of this space,” says Haynes. A Murphy bed is built into the bookshelves for additional sleeping space when the family has a full house, while the graphic sofa plays into the client’s adventurous side.

Designed to keep the couple’s daughter entertained through Chicago’s long winters, the playroom is equal parts imagination and movement. A modular foam sofa can be reconfigured into a fort or gymnastics landing pad, while a custom climbing wall encourages active play. Haynes’s favorite detail? A hand-crocheted saffron yellow swing that brings the home’s recurring signature hue into its most playful space.

Drama was on the menu in the dining room, where walls painted in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal meet a matching lacquered ceiling. Getting the glossy aubergine finish just right took several attempts, but the result was worth the trouble.

In the home’s soaring living room, Haynes tempered the airy architecture with a collection of tactile, earthy furnishings. Seafoam sofas dressed in block-printed pillows mingle with woven armchairs, a pair of travertine coffee tables, and a vintage African stool. The mix lends visual weight to the expansive space without sacrificing the comfortable, family-friendly atmosphere.

A deep archway visible from the front door offered the perfect opportunity for a little surprise. Haynes tucked a pair of custom banquettes around a circular dining table, upholstering the snug seating in a saffron-and-blue block print sourced from Etsy. The palette echoes the nearby reading room, while the clever configuration makes everyday meals feel wonderfully intimate.

Founded originally in 2018 as Kate Haynes Interiors and reborn in 2023 as Noble Studio, Kate Haynes has been honing a fascination with spaces, architecture, and art since childhood. Her company has always been about beautiful spaces (naturally), but the rebirth as Noble Studio comes with a sense of mature intentionality to create quiet luxury for clients. Kate brings to her team a relentless commitment to the intangible result of joy present in the aesthetics and flow of a place, no matter what style it wears.

#6780. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Many enterprise breaches still begin with familiar weaknesses, according to a new report from SonicWall.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

#6778. 6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6777. These Perfumes Are So Irresistible, Strangers Will Stop You to Ask What You’re Wearing

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Finding your signature scent involves a lot of trial and error—and a tingling nose. Fragrance is powerful, and discovering a unique scent for daily or occasional wear can uplift your mood, evoke a memory, or make your presence known in any room before you even speak. Whether you’re hunting for a fragrance that feels distinctly you or building a perfume wardrobe to ensure you’re prepared for every event on your social calendar, shopping for perfume can feel overwhelming. One moment, everyone’s going for guava; the next, it’s all about sea salt.

Truth is, trends come and go. However, the journey to building a unique collection is about finding a fragrance that truly resonates—whether floral, gourmand, musky, or a distinct layered blend.

“Fragrance has the power to evoke memories of a person, a moment, an experience,” says DSM-Firmenich perfumer Bérénice Watteau. “We’ve all experienced it: walking down the street, catching a familiar scent on a stranger, and suddenly being brought back to someone we love or a beautiful memory shared with them. Perfume captures those fleeting feelings and makes them timeless.” Your scent should also feel like an extension of yourself, something that reflects your personality, style, and the way you want to be remembered. Don’t be afraid to layer a couple of scents for an irreplicable fragrance of your own creation.

As beauty editors, we’re lucky enough to spritz, sniff, and test hundreds of scents, so it takes a lot for one to impress us—that’s how you know the ones on the list below are extra special. We also consulted three experts and combed through numerous customer reviews to find 16 of the best perfumes for women that we can’t stop wearing.

Have you ever wondered what Kim Kardashian’s hair smells like? Well, here’s your answer. She uses the hair mist version of the Valaya Exclusif Eau de Parfum. The formula delivers a fresh, clean finish, anchored by a beautifully balanced blend of bergamot, mandarin, and white peach. Plus, the tassel-adorned bottle looks beautiful on display.

Smells like: A Kardashian’s hair (literally)

Key notes: Bergamot, mandarin, white peach

It works great as a stand-alone, but works even better with any of your favorite scents.

ELLE editor review: “If I’m being honest, I rarely layer my scents, However, in the name of journalism, I tested out the Lametta Eau De Parfum both on it’s own and paire with a few of my other go-tos. No matter what notes it touched, the result was always alluring—especially when balancing out sweet or floral scents.”—EAB

Joan Tanis is the national training and events director at Maison Francis Kurkdjian.

Tynan Sinks is a New York City-based beauty writer and the co-host of fragrance podcast Smell Ya Later.

Bérénice Watteau is a fine fragrance perfumer at DSM-Firmenich

What should you consider when choosing a perfume?

Understanding fragrance notes is one of the most effective ways to find your perfect scent. “Very similar to the fashion industry, there can be trends with fragrances as well,” Tanis says. “Choosing a fragrance isn’t always easy, but make the right choice by selecting one that reflects who you are and makes you feel good. My best advice is to let your personality, tastes, and age guide your choice.”

“The most important part of finding a fragrance is, of course, trying it on,” Tanis continues, adding that you can have multiple signature scents, rather than just one, for variety. “Nowadays, it is all about a collection of fragrances. Much like clothing, modern men and women desire a fragrance wardrobe, allowing them the freedom to choose what fragrance to wear daily.”

What is the difference between an eau de toilette and eau de parfum?

Many popular scents fall into these fragrance categories. The difference between the two lies in the concentration of perfume oil each contains, says Tanis. “Eau de toilettes (EDT) have a relatively lighter composition due to their low concentration of perfume oil— between five and 15 percent. On the other hand, Eau de parfums (EDP) have a higher concentration of perfume oils—typically between 12 and 18 percent—meaning they offer a significantly stronger scent that lasts between five and eight hours.”

Choosing between the two might come down to the specific occasion or the weather (i.e., you may lean towards a lighter scent for work and during the summer months, and a richer scent for nights out and during the winter). Check out our perfume terminology guide to learn more about fragrance terms.

Tanis suggests applying fragrance to the pulse points, where the skin is naturally warm, such as the wrists, neck, or inside the elbows. “This will allow the fragrance to warm up and reveal the perfume's sillage (trail) over time.”
