#6775. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#6767. This California Cottage Puts a Charming Spin on the "Summer Camp Nostalgia" Aesthetic

Nostalgia rules in this 1930s Northern California home.

When Corine Maggio and her husband bought their 1930s cottage in Mill Valley, California, it was meant to be their starter home. Coming in at about 1,500 square feet, with an awkwardly laid-out kitchen, living room, and entryway, it didn’t seem to be an ideal place to raise their two children—that was, until they realized how rare it was to have a view, a yard, and walkability. Before they knew it, they were falling in love with their home—and all the potential it held.

“The house has an energy you can’t just recreate,” Maggio explains. With three bedrooms and two bathrooms, there was technically enough room for their family of four, and the architectural bones could easily meld with the relaxed, “summer camp in Maine” aesthetic Maggio chases in every project. “We needed to get smart about space planning and bring back a significant amount of character that had been stripped away,” she adds. In other words, the layout had to be revamped, and nearly every wall needed a dose of paneling, paint, or wallpaper—or preferably, some combination of the three.

The kitchen was first on the chopping block. Sandwiched between the laundry room and the dining room, the space felt cut off from the rest of the house in a way that wasn’t conducive to their family-focused, indoor-outdoor lifestyle. Maggio’s solution was to remove the laundry room wall, giving the kitchen access to the backyard, and finish the space with a painted beadboard ceiling to lend it a rustic, timeworn feel.

Mixed patterns, layered textures, and natural materials became Maggio’s way of capturing that summer camp nostalgia without creating a space that felt too formal for everyday life. The narrow living room lent itself well to a circular seating arrangement; when paired with a canopy-inspired wallcovering, the layout mirrored the act of sitting around a campfire to tell stories. Throughout, vintage furniture mingles with custom pieces, and personal touches abound, from the butcher block Maggio found on Facebook Marketplace to a chair left behind by a previous owner.

“I want people to come in and feel something familiar,” Maggio says. “I want them to feel comfortable being themselves, knowing nothing in the house is more precious than the people.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Corine MaggioLocation: Mill Valley, CaliforniaThe Space: A 1930 cottage with three bedrooms and two bathrooms.

Located in the center of the action—and boasting the best views of the water—this multifunctional space was designed as a place for family and guests to gather while Maggio and her husband cook.

After reviewing more than 30 wallpaper samples, Maggio finally selected this woodland scene by Bunny Williams Home, her least favorite of the bunch until she held it up in the space and saw the transformation it would make.

To visually lighten the galley kitchen, upper cabinets flanking the Ilve range were fitted with glass, breaking up the run of solid cabinetry and creating a more open feel. The butcher-block island is from the 1940s and weighs over 400 pounds.

Originally, there wasn’t a proper mudroom, only a three-walled entry open to the elements. Maggio enclosed it, painting the new beadboard-covered walls in Swans Mill Cream by Benjamin Moore, a cheery yellow that glows in the sunlight.

Maggio’s home office is a detached Tuff Shed in the backyard, to which she added paneling and Soane Britain wallpaper for an English garden vibe. Pinned-up magazine pages on the wall serve as both inspiration and art.

Maggio’s daughter, June, “personifies joy,” so she wanted her retreat to promote imagination and fun. Filled with vintage pieces and clad in a Pierre Frey wallpaper, it feels nostalgic and youthful.

To accentuate the room’s vaulted ceiling, Maggio switched from vertical nickel groove panels to horizontal ones, carrying them up to the room’s peak.

In a controversial move, the designer reduced the room’s square footage and opted for one sink instead of two. “We benefit significantly more from the counter space and storage,” she says. A vintage dresser gets new life as a vanity.

When Corine Maggio started her eponymous design firm, she knew she wanted to create homes for her clients that felt warm, character-rich, and deeply personal. Her goal isn’t to simply design spaces that look beautiful and collected, but to improve how people feel in their daily lives.

#6766. Our Last Chance to Save This Country Begins Today. Let’s Not Waste It.

Don’t even start thinking about the 2028 presidential election. Start thinking about what we’re going to do tomorrow and the day after that.

(Optional Video Accompaniment to This Post)

It is a decade now since the president came down the golden escalator and the country went straight to hell. All we’ve done as citizens of a self-governing democratic republic is squabble over the seating in the handbasket. Until Tuesday. Tuesday was nut-cutting time. Tuesday felt—probably rightly—like the beginning of an extended last chance that the country will have to spend every waking hour pursuing.

Just on Monday, the president closed out his demented road show with a weird address in front of one of his administration’s most conspicuous blunders: the Reflecting Pool. Meanwhile, the Supreme Court, with his carefully manufactured conservative majority, gave the boot to his latest attempt to ratfck the California elections, and the court did so without even a hint of an explanation. Then the president capped a rich, full day by bombing an Iranian island in the Persian Gulf, although I guess he went a little bananas on his little boutique website.

Mama said there’d be days like this. But she never said there’d be so many. And all the rest of them are on us. If I can offer one piece of advice it would be this: Ignore anything written that references the 2028 presidential election. If things break the way the polls say they will, there will be so much concentration required to do what we can to ameliorate the damage this administration has done and is capable of doing over the next two years. Starting the 2028 horse-race coverage this week would be yet another crime against poor, battered journalism. As the late Senator Edward Kennedy told a Democratic convention long ago:

Run through the tape, America. Finish the race.

#6763. This Designer’s Parisian Pied-à-terre Features a Palette As Bold As It Is Beautiful

A storied apartment found new life through beloved heirlooms and a reverence for the past.

Paris, in Garrow Kedigian’s telling, looks better in color. Not the cream, black, and gilt version that has come to dominate our collective imagination, but cognac, lacquered red, turquoise, and umber—the type of palette that makes a room feel alive before even a single antique arrives. “Traditionally, French design was very colorful,” Kedigian says, citing the richly layered interiors of François Catroux and Jacques Grange as antecedents. “It was only in the modern age that white and cream has just overtaken.”

His own Paris apartment, on Rue de Varenne in the 7th arrondissement, makes a persuasive case for the pigment of a bygone era. Located directly across from the Musée Rodin, the home is a step up—literally and figuratively—from Kedigian’s former pied-â-terre, a smaller space that proved a little too successful at attracting visiting friends and family. “I ended up giving them my bed, and then I’d have to sleep on the sofa,” he recalls. More time in Paris also meant more work there, and the designer wanted a proper desk, rather than an “office” that disappeared every time he cleared the dining table for guests.

What he found boasted grandeur in spades—14-foot ceilings, generous rooms, an enviable address—but had suffered through an unfortunate 1970s renovation. Kedigian, who has a background in classical architecture, set about putting the apartment back together, basing much of his approach on one of the few surviving details: a curved bay window and the kitchen’s shapely cabinetry. “That was the generator for the apartment,” Kedigian says. From there, he opened up sight lines, raised the doorways to 19 feet, restored missing moldings, and sourced period-appropriate details, like a Pompadour-style mantel, at Parisian flea markets.

His studied mix of restrained maximalism is on display in every corner. Antiques gathered across decades mingle with modern art, Venetian lighting, sculptural objects, and sentimental pieces from former homes. It’s a chemistry lab of sorts, a place Kedigian refers to as his “prototype,” where he can try the moves a client might initially resist. And yet, the result is undeniably cohesive, neither perfectly preserved nor particularly interested in behaving itself. As Kedigian puts it, “It’s mixing the things that you find most beautiful in a way that they work well together.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Garrow KedigianStylist: Robert RufinoLocation: Paris, FranceThe Space: A two-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom apartment across 1450 square feet

Affectionately dubbed the “Grand Salon,” this elegant space pays homage to all the things designer Garrow Kedigian loves about Parisian design, from the one-of-a-kind blue mixed for the walls to the intricate plaster molding and the custom tufted banquette that follows the window’s curve.

“The original plaster crown molding, fireplace mantle, and over mantle mirror really anchor the room,” says Kedigian. “My furniture layout plan was a derivative of the fireplace and curved window, including banquette seating organized around the fireplace.”

Kedigian prefers to work surrounded by red, though this custom Benjamin Moore paint shade is decidedly more “fiery” than the one in his New York City apartment. “The walls were a custom mixed color—I wanted a rich, enveloping red with a lot of energy, hence more towards the orange tones,” he adds. Built-ins anchor one side of the room, while large-scale dentil molding frames the space, adding contrast and texture.

This passageway is a main artery through the home, so Kedigian knew it needed to carry its weight. To make it purposeful, he relocated several doors and added a curved niche to spotlight favorite objets d’art.

Anchored by a vintage La Cornue stove (a discontinued colorway Kedigian found on Le Bon Coin, the French eBay), the blue and red kitchen nods to the French flag, giving Kedigian a stylish spot for pre-dinner cocktails (though, admittedly, not much cooking). “Kitchen should always be designed as an extension of your main living spaces; colors and materials should be carried throughout,” says Kedigian.

Spurred on by a bold black-and-mustard color palette—which a client of Kedigian’s fell for after seeing it in his space—the cozy dining nook leans into drama, backed by an expansive mirror and a gilded ceiling that bounce light around the room.

In what Kedigian calls his “greatest trick,” he painted double lines onto the walls to mimic the look of molding where there is none. A curtained 18th-century canopy bed brings historic flair.

Fulfilling his love for a bathroom that is “sultry and sexy,” Kedigian clad this space in a striking mix of black marble and gleaming brass sheets.

Born and raised in Montreal, interior designer Garrow Kedigian studied architecture at McGill University before honing his design sensibility under celebrated Boston designer William Hodgins. After relocating to New York City, he established his eponymous firm in 2001, building a reputation for richly layered interiors that marry classical architecture with bold color, sophisticated detailing, and contemporary flair. His portfolio spans residential and commercial projects across Manhattan, Greenwich, and Palm Beach, including his own storied Park Avenue apartment, once home to Fred Astaire.

#6762. The 4 Best Super Automatic Espresso Machines

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These robot baristas will do every little step for you—except latte art.

Here’s something I think someone out there needs to hear: Not everything should be easy. Not every problem should be outsourced to the robots. Oh, ChatGPT can make a podcast about your kids’ days. That’s a shortcut that leads you nowhere. One thing, however, can be totally handed over to the robots with no consequence. At-home coffee. You can make espresso a hobby, turn it into rocket science—some on our Esquire staff do just that. But most of us just want our morning latte or Americano, a caffeine fix to get us going. If that’s you, the best gift you can get yourself is a fully automatic espresso machine—or, as it’s known by its more fun name, a super automatic espresso machine.

I love my Breville grinder (it makes the task quick work every time), but some folks just want simplicity and a latte on demand. Can’t say I blame them. An onboard hopper and grinder is one of the boxes a super automatic espresso machine must check. The others? A digital screen that lets you select beverages, adjustable settings and user profiles, and, most importantly, it must do all the weighing and measuring for you, ensuring it brews the same drink every time. Many, but not all, come with milk frothers and tanks to hold your milk or milk alternative.

At their best, the process is as easy as filling it up with beans and milk and then letting the warm-up rinse cycle boot. After that, any hot coffee or espresso drink you want (and sometimes cold-brewed ones too) is available at the push of a button. Some add extra steps, but the core of it is no measuring, no tamping, no wasted time. And not a single wasted cup. From De’Longhi, KitchenAid, and Terra Kaffe, these are the best super automatic espresso machines at every price point that will brew you a consistent espresso every time.

If you’ve read about products on Esquire for any amount of time, you know that we stan De’Longhi. My editor Luke Guillory and I can attest: When we see Magnifica, we say “magnificent.” For the price, the Magnifica Duo is both an excellent value and also everything you really need in an automatic coffee machine. It comes with a milk tank equipped with a foaming wand and dispenser. That whole apparatus is completely dishwasher safe so you don’t have to worry about scrubbing a thing. You are buying an automatic espresso machine after all.

It doesn’t have an LCD touch screen, but the touch-sensitive buttons pair with a small screen to make drink selection take a matter of seconds. It can be a bit clunky at first, but getting used to it doesn’t take much time. From there, you can program quick selects to get straight to your favorite drinks in even less time. The machine cleans itself and, eventually, it will pay for itself too.

For me, the most important thing an espresso machine can automate is maintenance. I’m always forgetting to clean my coffee makers (among the dozens of other gadgets I need to keep clean), but I never have to worry about that with my KitchenAid. This super automatic espresso machine and iced coffee maker rinses the machine and milk tube routinely, and in the settings I can always see how close a part is to needing a cleaning. On top of that, it’s a super easy machine that makes tasty beverages. It doesn’t have the 50 plus drinks of a $2,000 De’Longhi, but you have most of your basics including cappuccinos, lattes, and even iced coffee and iced espresso.

The KF3 is the least expensive of KitchenAid’s line of super automatics because it doesn’t come with a milk tank. Instead, the AutoMilk system it does have is brilliant. Just stick the provided milk tube into any vessel and it will extract the proper amount of liquid into the dispenser tank above your cup, where it is steamed and frothed to the ideal temp. After your drink is ready, you’ll be prompted (but not forced!) to run a rinse of the milk tube. I recommend it; keep that stuff clean.

And finally, it’s worth mentioning this machine is made from PFAS-free parts. This means a much reduced risk to exposure to microplastics in your coffee. Everyone can appreciate that.

If having something that looks great on your counter while it does the bean grinding and shot pulling for you is the priority, you have to take a look at Terra Kaffe’s machines. The sleek, minimalist design says “retro future” without the rest of your kitchen needing to fit any specific vibe. You use a stainless steel dial to literally “dial in” your brew strength and temperature. It’s the perfect machine for those who drink a daily espresso or Americano.

I mention those drinks because, as any coffee drinker will know, they don’t contain milk. While it’s coming in at a nice price, the Demi does admittedly lack a milk foaming option. If you already own a milk frother at home, though, this might be a way to save some money and still get a fantastic, no-work espresso machine.

Here’s the tea. This is an older model—2023 is ancient by gadget standards—and they are getting more difficult to find in stock. At least this means the price has gone down... a little bit. The fact remains, every one of the coffee freaks on Reddit swears by the Eletta Explore. It is still the pinnacle of what De’Longhi’s super automatic machines can do, with dozens and dozens of hot and cold drink recipes at your very fingertips. The milk frothing tech is top notch and pretty much all of the parts are dishwasher safe for cleaning. It even includes a travel mug for those mornings when time isn’t on your side.

The only complaint I can see raised against it is that, at this price, many competitors have swappable bean hoppers. The $1,300 De’Longhi Rivelia has a bean swap system, where you can switch between two different bean hoppers—decaf drinkers love this for switching over in the afternoon. Users report mixed results since you still have to clear an unseeable number of beans from the grinder before the switch is complete. This can land you in a no-man’s-land where you might be drinking half-caf on accident. It’s why I’m still recommending the Eletta over that machine.

#6759. Flattering and Low-Maintenance, the Midi Bob With Bangs Is the Simple Way to Update Your Hairstyle

It’s the noncommittal way to embrace shorter lengths.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Short haircuts such as a pixie crop or a jaw-skimming bob never fail to make an impact, creating a beauty mood that feels instantly fresh while bringing out the very best of your natural hair texture.

But if you’re not ready to take the plunge or fancy a more gradual change, take a leaf out of Taylor Swift’s book with a “midi” bob and a fringe to add structure and interest. Swift spent a majority of the last few years with long hair, so getting incremental trims is not only a great way to remove any dry or split ends, but it also helps ease you into a shorter haircut.

“The midi bob sits in that sweet spot between a classic bob and longer hair. It usually falls somewhere between the jawline and collarbone, which gives it a softer, more lived-in feel than a sharper, shorter cut,” says Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons. “What I like about it is that it doesn’t feel too ‘done’ or too precious. Adding a fringe instantly brings another dimension to the shape. It frames the face, breaks up the length, and gives the cut more personality. It’s often that little detail that takes a simple bob and makes it feel individual.”

Hairstylist and founder of Arkive Headcare, Adam Reed, agrees, adding: “It’s the balance between something beautifully classic and something that feels incredibly modern. The bob is one of those haircuts that continually reinvents itself, and I think the midi length is particularly appealing because it doesn’t feel too restrictive.”

The best news about a midi bob with fringe is also the versatility. You can still have it up or down, and it can be easily tailored to every hair type and curl pattern. “The fringe can be adapted too,” adds Hersheson. “Whether that’s a curtain fringe, a softer bottleneck shape, or something fuller, it’s really about finding the version that works with your natural texture rather than fighting against it.”

As with all haircuts, the main consideration should be to understand how your hair naturally behaves. “A fringe sits front and center, so things like cowlicks, growth patterns, and texture matter,” says Hersheson.

He adds: “You also need to think about the relationship between the fringe and the rest of the cut. A bob can sometimes feel quite graphic, so I often prefer a fringe that has a bit of softness and movement through it. It keeps the overall look modern and wearable.”

If you’re new to fringes, try a longer curtain fringe or a soft side-swept style as they are more versatile, shares Reed. Fringes do require some maintenance and regular trimming, so tell your hairdresser to tailor it to your lifestyle. “My biggest piece of advice is to bring references, but allow your hairdresser to interpret them for your hair rather than trying to recreate somebody else’s fringe exactly,” Reed tells ELLE.

There is no one-size-fits-all when it comes to hair, but Hersheson shares his main tips to ensure a polished yet relaxed finish, no matter your hair type.

Curly hair: Don’t try to force curls into a bob shape they don’t want to be. Work with the natural movement and focus on creating a strong silhouette. The Almost Everything Cream and a bit of diffused drying will usually give you a much better result than over-styling. Fringes on curly hair can look brilliant when they’re slightly looser and less perfect.

Thick hair: It’s all about using the weight of your hair to your advantage. Internal layers can stop the shape from becoming triangular while still keeping that fullness people love. When styling, don’t over-smooth it. A little texture is what makes a midi bob feel modern.

Straight hair: This hair type spotlights the silhouette of a sharper bob, but if your hair is prone to looking flat, add texture through the ends or use a texture spray to take away that freshly styled finish. The goal is for it to look like you’ve lived in it for a day or two.

Fine hair: A midi bob can actually make fine hair appear much thicker because you’re concentrating the weight into a stronger shape. Keep products light and avoid anything too heavy around the roots. A soft fringe can create the illusion of fullness around the front and make the entire haircut feel more substantial.

#6755. Pastel Hues and Big Jewelry: Decoding The Gilded Age Season 4’s Fashion

The new season of The Gilded Age uses fashion as a powerful storytelling method.

Everyone’s favorite HBO period drama about New York high society at the turn of the 20th century is back for a fourth season. In The Gilded Age, all the main characters (played by Carrie Coon, Christine Baranski, Audra McDonald, Cynthia Nixon, and Morgan Spector) are back. But this time, their outfits are just a little bit different. As old and new money continue to clash, the gowns, jewelry, and glamour speak louder than ever before. The outfits also show where the characters are mentally, more than ever. Within the first episode, we’ve already seen marriage, birth, and daring tensions, all dramatically dressed and detailed.

For the series, costume designer Kasia Walicka-Maimone takes inspiration from the real changing fashion of New York City from the 1870s to the 1890s: a time when trends and silhouettes were rapidly evolving. Walicka-Maimone and her team look to paintings, photography, and fashion plates from the period to inform the grand gowns and refined suiting. “We are seeing more artistic license in the dress designs this season, with traditional elements mixed with modern ones like the sleeveless top Marian wore to the after-wedding party downtown at Webster Hall,” Elizabeth L. Block, author of the Gilded Age Fashion book and newsletter, tells ELLE. “The juxtapositions are fun, maybe even more so than the one-to-one matchups with actual Gilded Age dresses we saw in the earlier seasons.”

Even if we’re early in the season, it’s fascinating to see color tell such a story through clothing. Socialite sisters Agnes van Rhijn (Christine Baranski) and Ada (Cynthia Nixon) are at the center of the show, and in the new season, their looks reflect a slightly more joyful representation of their characters. Think: embroidered details and luscious hues. “Aunt Ada is loosening up,” adds Block. “She is fully out of mourning for her husband, and we are seeing her in lighter color combinations like pink and green. For a while last season she was all buttoned up in black and deep blues.” Gleaming buttons and lace trims allude to the fact that these sisters have access to the best of the best textiles.

On the same wavelength, Peggy (Denée Benton) and her mother, Mrs. Scott (Audra McDonald), demonstrate through their clothing that they have money, but most importantly, taste. There’s a short conversation about Brussels bobbin lace that is quite revealing, however. (Consuelo Vanderbilt famously wore a Brussels lace veil for her glorious 1895 wedding to the Duke of Marlborough.) Their highly ornate outfits with richly detailed trims and embellishments are unrivaled.

Still, sisters Agnes and Ada are more toned down than someone like Bertha Russell (Carrie Coon) and her railroad tycoon husband George Russell (Morgan Spector) or Peggy and her family, who have all always used clothing as armor and a communication tool. Despite seeing Bertha dressed down early in the episode during Gladys’s (Taissa Farmiga) giving birth, she later comes to fruition with diamond tiaras, puffed and beaded sleeves, and embellished full-skirted ball gowns.

A major fashion moment comes when Enid Winterton (Kelley Curran) and Oscar van Rhijn (Blake Ritson) get married. Enid wears an opulent gown embroidered with flowers in a soft shade of pinkish lavender, with matching gloves and an ornate pink floral headpiece to match. As Block says, it’s a “pinky-lavender dress for a lavender marriage,” and “The ensemble immediately reads as incongruous but elegant and playful, like the two characters, who are now fan favorites.” With a flash of pale green and a long train, the look is a nod to the stereotype of the idea of new money being flashier than old money. Enid’s wedding look is the equivalent of a fashion debut for her in a way, and later on, we see her in a pink silk trimmed with sumptuous fur, accented with a pearl necklace and earrings.

We also see Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) emerge even more as an example of a young woman who’s interested in dressing differently. Her vivid mint green and black striped gown with draped details was incredibly visually striking and felt new and bold, while her matching hat felt like a nod to the kind of more mature, old-money style of her aunts. She also wears a blue lace gown with velvet shoulder details and matching opera gloves, revealing more skin than the old-money characters in the show.

If you’re a jewelry (or accessories) lover, it’s the season to watch. Enid’s spectacular diamond necklace and whimsical approach to hats covered in bows and feathers are gasp-worthy and definitely among the most entertaining parts of the show. In The Gilded Age season 4, the fashion is vital to the drama.

#6754. Reggie Dinkins Is Still the Funniest Show on TV

Tracy Morgan and Daniel Radcliffe return for season 2 of their hilarious NBC sitcom somehow even wittier than before.

It’s a gross exaggeration to say that television is a lost art, but we can maybe agree that it has changed. Big-time. But sadly, not all of it is for the better. The binge model pigeonholes every show into a novel, scriptwriters tragically factor in second-screen doomscrolling, and the half-hour situational comedy has been driven to near extinction. Which is why I thank the television gods for The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins and then thank them again that it’s back so soon.

Still sharp, still absurd, and still just plain funny, The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins proves sophomores aren’t always in a slump, with a new season that picks up from season 1’s high point. “More of the same” isn’t bad news here, and if the series can keep pace, we might have a proper TV MVP when other sitcoms can’t make the cut.

Season 2 of Reggie Dinkins, which premiered November 2 on NBC, catches up with Daniel Radcliffe’s anguished auteur, Arthur Tobin, as he documents the show’s hero, Reggie Dinkins (Tracy Morgan). Dinkins is a disgraced football superstar, and he’s hell-bent on rehabilitating his public profile. Arthur still lives in the Dinkins’ crowded New Jersey mansion that also houses Reggie’s levelheaded ex-wife and manager Monica (Erika Alexander), their son Carmelo (Jalyn Hall), Reggie’s younger fiancée Brina (Precious Way), and Bobby Moynihan as Rusty, Reggie’s freeloading BFF who is too integral to the family’s cohesion to kick to the curb.

This season, Arthur grows ever closer to the Dinkins clan, despite his own hyperawareness of his documentary ethics violations. (Episode 2 of this season at last interrogates how compromised the in-progress project has been since the start.) Most of the ensemble, Radcliffe and Morgan included, exists in a sort of stasis—this is a network sitcom, after all—but it’s Precious Way who shines as the much-improved standout of the bunch.

Formerly “just” the younger woman in Reggie’s life last season, Brina has grown into the soul of the show. Along with Way’s buoyant charisma, there’s a newfound depth to Brina that wasn’t present in her before. I’ll admit, I thought she was grating at first blush, and I was ready to write her off as a shallow antagonist who didn’t seem involved with Reggie or the family in earnest. But in season 2, she’s evolved into a phenomenal supporting player who instantly brightens the room and contrasts beautifully with the others, most of all Radcliffe’s flummoxed British artiste. (A throwaway gag involving her “Voice Bucket Challenge” had me cracking up at my desk.) It’s a mere single component upgrade we’re talking about here, but it elevates Reggie Dinkins to a much higher degree this time around.

Jalyn Hall, too, grows in his once-thankless role as Reggie and Monica’s teenage son, Carmelo. Through a new overarching storyline involving a forbidden romance, which becomes a linchpin for most of season 2’s first half, Carmelo crystallizes in his specific variant of “Blerd” that is more refreshing than derivative. He’s not just “The Geek,” as you’d find in most sitcoms. He’s fluid enough to perform the function without killing the buzz.

The first four episodes of the season, provided to the press, keep up season 1’s winning formula—22-minute-sitcom hijinks that unfold against an airy atmosphere with well-timed punchlines. Daniel Radcliffe and Tracy Morgan further assert their powers as TV’s unlikeliest duo, and I can’t get enough of them. I pray there are yet more improbable excuses to ensure multiple seasons of a single documentary’s production. Sure, the rhythms are familiar now, but it’s welcome fun. Reggie Dinkins is a potent reminder of what television used to be and what it still can be if we’re brave enough to let it.

#6753. 15 Paint Colors HGTV Stars Swear By for a Home That Looks Expensive

From sea blue to crisp white, these designer favorites are timeless.

Everyone knows that a quick coat of paint is the fastest way to transform a room. It’s a favorite trick among professional designers and DIYers alike, but choosing the perfect shade isn't always easy. With hundreds of colors to choose from, finding “the one” can quickly become overwhelming. If you’re feeling a little stuck, don’t fret: HGTV’s biggest stars make these tough decisions every day.

Whether they’re renovating an outdated home or putting the finishing touches on a dream build, HGTV personalities are constantly selecting paint colors that make a space feel fresh, spacious, inviting, and beautifully designed. Over the years, they’ve crowned a select few as go-to favorites that are foolproof and versatile enough to work in almost any room. When every renovation comes with deadlines and budgets, dependable paint colors become invaluable.

To help narrow your search, we asked some of our favorite HGTV stars to reveal the paint colors they reach for most often. Their picks include everything from versatile neutrals to powdery pastels that work across a variety of homes and aesthetics. Additional copy by Alyssa Khan.

“We love muted colors because they’re playful, but not over-the-top. Behr Mocha Ice is a perfect example of that balance—it brings the personality you need to take a space from basic and bland to warm and interesting. Try it in a bedroom, dining room, or anywhere that’s missing that ‘wow’ factor.”—Drew Scott, Property Brothers

“This beautiful blue is timeless and versatile. It’s calming, but not boring. Even though Behr's Dragonfly makes a statement, it’s still neutral enough for many spaces, whether it’s on every wall in a bedroom or used on a specific feature like kitchen cabinets.”—Jonathan Scott, Property Brothers

“This never-boring black paint color is dramatic and impactful yet very versatile. Pair with white for a timeless aesthetic. Use it to create a dramatic accent wall or outside for your exterior (like we did) or trim for maximum curb appeal.”—Tarek El Moussa, The Flip Off

“My favorite paint color is Tricorn Black by Sherwin-Williams. It’s bold, clean, and makes every detail pop. Whether it’s a door, a wall, or literally anything else, this black brings that high-end look every single time. Never fails.”—Cole DeBoer, Down Home Fab

“I am currently loving greige, the perfect blend of gray and beige. It’s warm, soft, and timeless—a great option for homeowners who want both cozy and modern. Benjamin Moore's Revere Pewter is a classic and rich greige with warmth. Great for open-concept homes.”—Heather Rae El Moussa, The Flip Off

“What I love most about it is its confidence. Studio Green by Farrow & Ball doesn’t whisper: It commands. It’s deep, moody, and unapologetically rich, the kind of color that instantly elevates a space and gives it a point of view. In daylight, it reads as a classic, almost architectural green, grounded and refined. But at night? It transforms into something velvety and dramatic, wrapping the room in a quiet luxury that feels both intimate and powerful.”—Egypt Sherrod, Married to Real Estate andProperty Virgins

“Benjamin Moore Misty Air OC-44 is my constant when I need a bright neutral. It is a broken-in cream that’s the perfect backdrop for colorful art and upholstery.”—Erin Napier, Home Town

““My current favorite paint color is Black Fox by Sherwin-Williams. It’s the perfect mix of sophisticated and moody, with just enough warmth to make any space feel grounded and timeless. It pairs beautifully with so many other tones and textures. I love it in an office!”—Chelsea DeBoer, Down Home Fab

Magnolia Home by Joanna Gaines Juniper Tree Crafted by KILZ

“Seed & Supply was one of the first additions we made to the Silos back in 2015. Before it served as our garden shop on the grounds, it was used as an additional retail space at the original Magnolia shop, which we lovingly refer to as The Little Shop on Bosque. This year, the team had the idea to evolve the building yet again, but this time to serve as a creative workshop space for our guests. I immediately loved the idea. It has always been my hope that our guests’ experience at the Silos would inspire creativity and connection.

The whole interior of the workshop is refreshed with one color, Juniper Tree Trim, Door, and Cabinetry Paint in satin finish. I love how these nature-forward hues turned out—they feel fresh yet familiar, bold yet comforting.”—Joanna Gaines, Fixer Upper

““White Flour is the perfect soft white backdrop for every style. It accentuates simplicity or maximalist energy. The warm hue is anything but basic.”—Taniya Nayak, Battle on the Beach

“I love Farrow & Ball's Drop Cloth for walls, or for trim and cabinetry with a more true white on the wall. It is a timeless neutral that looks beautiful, particularly in historic homes. This warm neutral evokes calm tranquility without being sterile. It is the perfect first layer for any space: It sets a foundation of simplicity that can easily be layered upon with color on cabinetry, trim detail, furniture, art, and décor.”—Jenny Marrs, Fixer to Fabulous

“I keep going back to Behr White. It’s porcelain-like and a classic white without being too stark or jarring. It also has a very slight undertone of blue, which I prefer over yellow or pink undertones, as a slight hint of blue preserves more of a pure white tone.”—Galey Alix, Home in a Heartbeat With Galey Alix

“My current favorite color is Benjamin Moore Windsor Green. I just painted my bedroom this color! The walls, the doors, the windows, and the trim. It's just so deep and lovely. To me, a deep mossy green is cozy and sophisticated at the same time. It steals the show every time, which is why I went for it in my own bedroom!”—Kristina Crestin, Farmhouse Fixer

In her blog for the Laurel Mercantile, the Home Town Takeover co-host crowned Dover White her all-time favorite hue.

“My all-time favorite white, forever and always, to the point my design team just defaults to it when they aren't able to get a quick decision from me.”— Erin Napier, Home Town Takeover

Fix My Flip star, Page Turner, shared her favorite shade of white with HGTV fans. Boasting warm cream and soft beige undertones, Whisper White is the perfect airy neutral.

“I like how this shade isn’t as stark as other white paints can be.” — Page Turner, Fix My Flip

The Property Brothers named Grenada Villa as one of their go-to shades for their site, drew & jonathan, but fans of Brother vs. Brother will recall the shade being name-dropped much earlier. Drew used it to coat the guest bedroom ceiling in the Season 6 Canyon House in 2018. The serene shade has remained in their palette ever since.

#6751. Martine Rose Is Balenciaga’s New Creative Director—and the First Woman to Lead the House

The London designer consulted on menswear at the brand under Demna’s tenure.

Balenciaga is entering a new era with a familiar face. Martine Rose, the British-Jamaican designer who served as a menswear consultant under Demna at the brand, is officially taking over the post following Pierpaolo Piccioli’s departure. The news was confirmed by Balenciaga’s parent company, Kering, on Friday.

“It is a true privilege to be appointed Creative Director of Balenciaga, a House with an extraordinary heritage and a fearless creative spirit,” Rose said in a statement. “Balenciaga has been shaped by remarkable creative talents before me. I am committed to honoring this legacy and look forward to creating the next chapter in this rich and beautiful story.”

While Rose might be a new face to the mainstream, she’s something of an industry darling among those in the know. Born in South London, she launched her namesake menswear label in 2007, drawing on 1990s counterculture and youth scenes, hip-hop, reggae, and football. Her design philosophy challenges conventional ideas of masculinity and gives familiar wardrobe staples an unexpected twist: She might stretch a suit’s proportions or turn sportswear into something sharper and more formal.

Rose, however, is more than a cult designer. Her clothes have found fans in Hailey Bieber, Timothée Chalamet, and Rihanna. In 2021, she sold a 60 percent stake in her brand to London-based fashion accelerator Tomorrow and has sidestepped the traditional fashion calendar for more community-focused showings. Her appointment at Balenciaga brings her back to the house and is also history-making: She is the first woman to lead the brand creatively.

From 2015 to 2018, Rose was brought on by Demna as a menswear consultant, helping launch Balenciaga’s first menswear collections and shape the oversized proportions and brutalist, severe aesthetic that became a signature of his tenure. Her influence on contemporary menswear has earned her the moniker “Your Favorite Designer’s Favorite Designer” among insiders.

“Demna was so brave in giving me a real shot,” Rose told British Vogue in 2021. “Up until that point, I was basically squatting,” she said. “It gave me the chance to actually do something, to put it all back into my own label and make a proper jump forward. It also gave me confidence.”

“Balenciaga has always been a house of radical creativity, challenging conventions and redefining the boundaries of fashion,” Luca de Meo, CEO of Kering, explained. “Martine Rose embodies this spirit with a fresh, fearless, and deeply contemporary vision. Her connection to the new generation and her instinctive understanding of culture bring a new dimension to the house. Her appointment reflects our ambition to keep Balenciaga at the forefront of innovation, creativity, and culture.”

Rose will be based in Paris and begin her role on November 16; the date of her first show has not yet been announced.

#6749. The 2026 Esquire Cologne Awards

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re looking to make a big impression on a special occasion or just searching for a great everyday scent, these winners have you covered.

There’s never been a better time to be a guy who wants to smell good. The men’s fragrance market is booming, the scents are bolder than ever, and you can shop for pretty much anything you could possibly imagine—from long-lasting extracts for a big night out to easy-to-wear options that’ll fit right in at the office—with ease.

The only problem with all that abundance is the project of narrowing things down to a manageable number of scents that might work for you. Which is where we come in. This year, the team here at Esquire decided to go big for our annual Cologne Awards. Our fragrance experts called in hundreds of new releases over the course of the year, narrowing them down through extensive testing and internal debate before each creating their own short list of winners.

That’s when things got really interesting. Round by round, the Esquire Cologne Awards committee met to discuss potential winners, spritzing and testing again and again before finally producing the list you see here. These are the 40 best scents of the year. You might not connect with every one of them—but if you can’t find something you love here, well, good luck out there.

This has become one of our daily wears—and for good reason. It’s going to please pretty much anyone. Spicy and aromatic, this cologne is vanilla and amber forward with discernible notes of Iris germanica and lavender giving it a more floral edge. The result is sensual and soft but never unmasculine, thanks to the aromatic freshness. Plus, it comes packaged in a giant flask. There’s nothing manlier than having a giant flask (literally) of cologne behind your bathroom mirror.

Leather lovers, this one is for you. Tom Ford’s newest Ombré Leather Reserve blends notes of earthy, black leather with silver juniper and sandalwood, creating a balanced, rooted, yet refreshing take on Ford’s classic Ombré Leather eau de parfum. Inspired by the great American West and the mountains and deserts that constitute it, this scent is a perfect balance between refinement and rugged masculinity.

If you were around in the ’90s and spent some time at the mall, you probably remember Gap Dream. It was the most iconic scent of the original lineup, which was discontinued in the early aughts. But Gap has given the people what they want: a relaunch of its beloved fragrance line. Dream remains the hero of the collection, fresh and clean like a brand-new white T-shirt, with bright citrus, soft florals, and botanical musk.

Noble but knows how to fuck. A raw oud with fresh sensuality. The peach is jam-like, adding a softness to the suede accord. Velvet Mood is a leather fragrance with added warmth, texture, and juicy sweetness. (It recalls the intimate, sexual encounter in Call Me by Your Name. The one with the peach. You know it.) It’s captivating and aura-maxxing, not a blind buy for most. But we’re still on the side of biting the peach.

This cologne has one job, and that job is beach. It’s salty, woody, and as fresh as anything else we smelled this year. From the blond driftwood to the beach rose, coconut water, and sea salt, all the notes are reminiscent of tropical paradise. It may not be summer anymore, but the perfect summer scent has landed. Now that it’s in your possession, you can turn any day into a beach day.

Though it is totally within the Ranger Station wheelhouse of western-inspired cowpoke smells, it’s a new earthy floral. The easy choice would have been to mix sandalwood and ambergris with whiskey and call it another saloon smell. Instead the Ranger Station team gave it an iris root heart—soft and floral without losing its edge. It’s not betting the farm and drinking away the loss. It’s betting the farm, losing it, and stepping out those double swing doors to see a sunrise and smell a new dewy and green morning. You lost it all, but that was yesterday. Keep moving forward and the next win can’t be too far down the road.

It took four years to get right and was, according to the company, the last thing on which Giorgio Armani put his personal stamp of approval. And it’s not what you might expect if you’re thinking of the aquatic, mineral freshness of something like Acqua di Giò. I Will is a different animal—all wood and vanilla with bergamot to keep things from getting too dark and dense. It’s rich and modern and exactly what people will be talking about 20 years from now when they’re discussing the Italian fashion house’s other iconic scents.

Soft and sensual, Musc Pallida lingers like a whisper on your skin. Powdery iris and warm musk are the main components here, and they work together seamlessly. It’s light and airy but doesn’t fade quickly, and it smells good on pretty much anyone. Don’t go into Musc Pallida expecting your typical manly musk fragrance—it’s much more elegant and intimate.

Earthy sweetness, earthly delights. This fragrance from Byredo is sweet without ever venturing into gourmand territory. Even with its Madagascar vanilla and vanilla liquor accord at the base, it is punctuated with fougères and florals that give it complexity without steering it into being a spice bomb or a cognac cocktail. Instead it has a bright greenness and soft musk that can see the light of day and what’s to come. Simply fresh, refined with some sweetness—for a beautiful day.

A sophisticated blend of cinnamon, sage, and bergamot gives this fragrance a warm, spicy character. Cinnamon fragrances tend to be overpowering and reserved for nighttime, but this scent feels sophisticated and is a great daytime spice since the spicy top notes are balanced by a vanilla and praline base. It’s an elegant take on a spice-forward scent and a Nordstrom exclusive, which makes sense since Parfums de Marly is one of the retailer’s top-selling brands.

Creed’s Iris Debonair—a totally new offering, not a new expression—walks a wonderful line. It’s very gentlemanly with its cool and classic fougère heart. The opening and its transition into the middle is a lot of bergamot, absinthe, and lavender, making it seem like an expected (albeit exceptional) barbershop scent. But as it settles, iris starts to come to the forefront. It’s not the old-fashioned iris of Green Irish Tweed but a softer one that pairs well with a soft ambrette dry down. It has a true masculine and a true feminine side, and neither controls your lasting memory of the scent. Totally captivating.

Citrus and amber are not natural bedfellows, but this scent—a more highly concentrated version of the former Le Gemme Tygar—makes a very strong case for their marriage. It’s bright and grapefruit-y in one instant and then rich and woody in another. The more you smell it, the more the moniker starts to make sense. It is, after all, named after tigereye, a gemstone revered not for its sameness but for the striking impression that its contrasting bands of color create.

Fig fragrances are having a moment, but this new Granado EDP blows all other fig scents out of the water. Candied green fig reveals rich, jammy notes that are sweet but not in the typical syrupy way; it’s a lush, even fresh kind of sweet. Once you get hints of sweet dates, florals, and woods, it all combines to create a beautifully creamy gourmand.

It opens fruity and floral. A burst of raspberry gives way to peony, geranium, jasmine, magnolia. That might sound a little tough for some guys—but remember it’s just the beginning. Those notes soon give way to a base of deep, powdery musks. It’s not your typical masculine fragrance; it’s something a little more delicate and, though the scent is rooted in centuries of Chinese tradition, modern. We’d encourage you to give it a try. You might just find a new daily driver.

Before you spritz this woody scent, you have to admire the artistry of the bottle. Mind Games is known for its chess-inspired bottles, and Knight’s Tour is a great example of that. The scent inside is a sophisticated blend of lavender and saffron with spicy accents up top and smooth woods at the base. It’s a great go-to option for every day.

One Esquire editor made Ripe her scent of the summer after it launched in the spring. The reason? Its perfect balance of warm florals, fresh fruity notes, soft woods, and musks is lush and enticing. Have you ever thought about how notes of lychee, watermelon sorbet, green leaves, rose water, and creamy sandalwood work together? Spoiler alert: It’s pretty awesome.

Ojai is like waking up in a luxury hotel in California, sunlight peeking through the shades as fruity and floral notes dance around the room. Juicy, ripe mango and honey-like saffron are at the heart of this fragrance, giving it a sweet yet elevated quality. But it also has an earthy, amber-y depth to it, thanks to patchouli and frankincense at the base.

Put rhubarb in a fragrance and we’re there. Demon Dancer takes the tartness of rhubarb and blackcurrant and adds bright citrus, fresh peppermint, and radiant orange blossom for an exciting opening before it settles into a woody base of amber, musk, and tonka bean. When the cool, fresh top and heart notes reveal the warm base, it really is something to take in.

Imagine that feeling when you’re walking around a smog-choked city and find an unexpected oasis. It’s fresh, green, but with just that tiniest hint of something darker and deeper. That’s this scent. It’s wood and pepper and greenery and a reminder that even in the midst of urban sprawl, we can always find a way back to nature.

The Dolce & Gabbana classic is now available as an alcohol-free perfume gel, making it a travel-friendly option for sensitive skin. The curved metal applicator makes it easy to apply the fragrance directly to pulse points, while the pocket-sized packaging is perfect for on-the-go touch-ups. The gel formula also works as a great base for layering if you already own the original fragrance and want to extend the wear time of the cult-favorite citrus scent.

#6748. Yeah, I’ll Try the Red-Light Therapy Vibrator

It’s been a big few years for red-light therapy. You can get it in a skin-clearing LED face mask, a hair-growth-stimulating cap, or a sauna blanket you can self-zip into like a burrito, and I’ve cheered on every new format. I just assumed we’d eventually run out of places for it. Oh, but reader, turns out there was one place left.

LELO, the Swedish luxury sex toy brand, just put red LEDs inside a G-spot vibrator called the MONA Spectra, and the brand says you can get the benefits of red-light therapy (collagen! circulation!) along with, you know, the main event.

As someone who reads the ingredient list on a $14 serum before it gets anywhere near my face, I had questions. So yes, I tried the red-light therapy vibrator. Here's my honest review, plus what the science actually says about red-light therapy for your vagina.

At its core, the MONA Spectra is a G-spot vibrator. It borrows the curved silhouette of LELO's longtime MONA line, with a rounded tip angled toward the front wall of the vagina (aka G-spot territory) and soft, body-safe silicone wrapped in twisted ribs for extra texture. It has 10 vibration settings and 16 intensity levels, and it's fully waterproof, so the bath is fair game.

The twist (besides the literal twisted ribs) is a built-in array of red LEDs. Out of the box, it runs red light at 615 to 625 nanometers, and the LELO app unlocks near-infrared, blue and green light, plus extra vibration patterns.

If you've ever bought an LED face mask, you already know the pitch. Red light is thought to help your cells repair themselves, which is why it's become such a skin-care staple, and LELO is applying the same idea to vaginal tissue.

LELO says regular use may support collagen and elastin, tissue elasticity, natural lubrication, and sensitivity. The brand also says the LED array gives off a gentle warmth that can relax the pelvic floor muscles and increase blood flow, which, for the record, your body tends to appreciate during sex anyway.

The pitch, basically, is folding a wellness habit into something you might already be doing.

The short answer: it's promising, but it's early. Most of the research on red light and vaginal health has focused on menopause, when dropping estrogen can lead to dryness and thinner, more sensitive tissue. A 2019 review of the research so far concluded that red-light therapy appears to be safe and may help with those symptoms, but the authors called the evidence preliminary, and none of it was testing an at-home vibrator.

LELO ran its own four-week survey with 37 women, and nearly 8 in 10 of those using it a few times a week reported noticeably more natural moisture, while close to 70% said they felt more confident in their bodies. It's an encouraging start, and bigger studies will tell us more. Until then, LELO itself positions the MONA Spectra as an everyday self-care device rather than a medical one, which is the right way to think about it.

So for now, think of the red light as a nice extra. If you're dealing with dryness or pain during sex, talk to your OB-GYN, who can help you figure out what will actually make a difference.

If you've used a LELO vibrator before, the MONA Spectra will feel familiar. It has the same gently curved shape and soft, smooth silicone as the regular MONA, and the twisted ribs add a little extra texture. The vibrations are what you'd expect from LELO, with enough settings to keep things low-key or turn them all the way up.

The one difference is warmth: with the LEDs on, the toy gets a little warm, which LELO's instructions say is normal.

If you're buying it for the red-light benefits, give it time. LELO's own survey ran for four weeks, and the brand recommends sticking to 15 minutes of light a day.

Yes (which makes the price easier to swallow). The lights are optional, so if the red light ends up being the thing you skip, you still own a high-end G-spot vibrator, and a very good one at that.

From what researchers have seen so far, yes, as long as you're using a device designed for intimate use and following its instructions. The MONA Spectra checks those boxes: it's made of body-safe silicone that meets ISO 3533, the international safety standard for sex toys, and LELO says its LEDs are UV-free, so you're not exposing sensitive skin to the kind of light that causes damage. A few ground rules from LELO:

If you're pregnant or managing a health condition, check with your doctor before adding any light therapy device to your routine, and read LELO's user manual for the full list of warnings.

If you already have a red-light routine and you've been eyeing a nicer G-spot vibrator anyway, the MONA Spectra makes a lot of sense. If you mostly want a great G-spot vibe and the light therapy is a maybe, LELO's regular MONA has the same familiar shape for a lot less money.

Yes. LELO says it's 100% waterproof and safe for the bath or shower.

LELO says the app is anonymous and fully encrypted, and you don't need it to use the toy's basic settings.

$299 is a lot of money for a vibrator, no way around it. It's also in the same price range as plenty of red-light face masks, and none of those double as a sex toy. The MONA Spectra is a seriously good G-spot vibrator, and the red light is a fun bonus for anyone who's already bought into the wellness of it all. If you haven't, stick with a regular LELO vibrator and save the money.

#6745. How Club Kid Fashion Went From the Dance Floor to the Runway

The underground scene’s DIY, gender-bending style still shapes how we get dressed today.

For his fall/winter 2009 runway show, “The Horn of Plenty,” Alexander McQueen offered a satirical critique of fashion’s throwaway culture. Models navigated a runway strewn with glass and discarded props in houndstooth looks and silhouettes that mocked fashion’s excess. Their alabaster-white painted faces and exaggerated lips evoked Leigh Bowery, the Australian performance artist known for his audacious club looks. “It’s a sackable offense, this collection,” McQueen said post-show. “I want people to look at it and say, ‘What the hell is that?’”

Much like McQueen upended fashion with his confrontational approach to design, Club Kids—a name given to the scene’s most flamboyant clubgoers, Bowery included—did the same in cities like New York and London. The scene was rife with larger-than-life, sometimes debaucherous personalities expressing themselves through equally outsized clothes; no look was too outlandish, no party too wild. It was that DIY spirit, championed by the likes of Susanne Bartsch, a nightlife impresario, RuPaul, and the infamous Michael Alig, that caught on beyond the clubs, creating an inextricable connection between nightlife and fashion. “You didn’t have to wait for a designer or a magazine to tell you what was fashionable,” Bartsch tells ELLE. “You could create a look, walk into the room, and see what happened. The crowd became the show, and the dance floor became a runway with no fixed rules.”

“Susanne, when she moved to New York, she opened a boutique and was the first person to carry clothes by [John] Galliano and [Jean Paul] Gaultier, [really] introducing that type of style into the city,” explains Ryan Burke, a New York-based makeup artist. “And then she also threw these parties; it went really hand in hand.”

Bartsch’s legendary parties at Savage and the Copacabana brought together a true amalgamation of worlds, from downtown denizens to fashion heavyweights like Thierry Mugler and Gaultier. (Like McQueen, Gaultier referenced Bowery in a 1991 runway show.) It was not only an intermixing of punchy, confident aesthetics, but also the idea that fashion can be an escape and the ultimate form of self-expression. My club nights in the ’80s brought together artists, designers, photographers, performers, and kids creating their own looks,” Bartsch notes. “For someone who has felt judged or excluded, that can be powerful. The [dancefloor] gives you permission to try something on not just an outfit, but a version of yourself.”

Meanwhile, in London, the Blitz Kids channeled that same appetite for theatrical self-expression through their DIY, gender-bending looks, which would influence collections by Marc Jacobs (most famously, his grunge collection for Perry Ellis), McQueen, and Anna Sui.

The AIDS crisis took an enormous toll on the communities that made New York nightlife pulse. Then came New York’s tough-on-crime crackdown, which helped drive many of the most popular clubs out of business. Today, the Club Kids’ heyday has the glow of downtown legend: a moment when self-expression ruled the dance floor and anyone could make an entrance. Now, Burke notes that social media has changed how people create and share their looks, making the heyday of the Club Kids feel all the more singular. (In film, the scene’s legacy has been in 2003’s Chloë Sevigny-starring Party Monster and is now slyly resurfacing in Jordan Firstman’s new film, Club Kid, where he plays a club promoter whose life is upended by the arrival of the 10-year-old son he never knew he had.)

“People are really crafty, really crafty online; they create, document it, take it off,” he says. “That’s cool too; any kind of way people want to express themselves is great, but there was something that was really magical about seeing people out. They tried more interesting things that might not work or might not look that good, but they tried it.”

The Club Kid spirit still reverberates in the underground scenes of New York, London, and beyond—and at the highest levels of fashion. You just need to know where to look.

During the recent spring/summer 2027 season, when many designers celebrated the “joy of getting dressed,” the club was never too far away. At Junya Watanabe, pearls and punk hardware were stacked atop catsuits with the carefree abandon of the original club kids, who built their looks from punk boutiques and thrift-store finds. Diesel got hot and heavy: Glenn Martens’s swan song for the brand unfolded amid a crowd of performers kissing and touching, while models wore barely-there denim and clothes that looked hastily thrown on, finished with smudged red lipstick, as if they’d been smooching on the dance floor. And August Barron showed a starlet-inspired lineup of glitzy “going out” dresses, sandals with neon heels, and haphazardly layered separates.

It’s easy to see why fashion keeps returning to the club: Nowhere else makes getting dressed feel quite so alive. As Bartsch puts it: “For me, the club spirit isn’t one particular look, it’s freedom, imagination and the confidence to express yourself without asking permission.”

#6744. Patrick Stewart Was a Serious Watch Guy. Here Are 5 of the Late Hollywood Legend's Best.

From a treasured Rolex to his beloved IWC, the late actor had a lifelong appreciation for the art of watchmaking.

“Someone once told me that time was a predator that stalked us all our lives," begins Captain Jean-Luc Picard, played by Sir Patrick Stewart in Star Trek Generations, in one of his most memorable lines. "I rather believe that time is a companion who goes with us on a journey, reminds us to cherish every moment, because it will never come again.”

Those musings on time feel particularly poignant now, following the British actor's death. There’s something rather comforting about the idea of time as a companion, particularly when you consider Stewart’s lifelong fascination with the very objects we use to measure it.

Because, as it turns out, Patrick Stewart was quite the watch guy. Not simply someone who happened to wear an expensive timepiece on the red carpet, but a genuine enthusiast with an appreciation for the craftsmanship, history, and ingenuity behind them. He bought his first watch at 18 and, by his own admission, never threw one away.

“The watch industry never ceases to amaze me,” he told the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie in 2019. “Because a watch is more than just an object that gives the time. Behind every watch there is an idea, a design, a concept, an innovation, and the watchmaker's desire to create something that is greater than anything that has ever existed before.”

It's an enthusiasm that stayed with him throughout his life, from the Rolex he reportedly spent almost all his savings on after arriving in Hollywood to his enduring affection for IWC Schaffhausen. And, naturally, there were some rather lovely watches along the way.

So, in celebration of an extraordinary career and a life well lived, we've taken a look back at some of Patrick Stewart's finest watch moments.

Perhaps the most meaningful watch in Stewart's story is one for which we don't have an exact reference. Long before he became one of Hollywood's most recognizable actors, Stewart was given a rather unexpected piece of career advice by the actress Eva Marie Saint. She advised him that appearances mattered, and that an expensive watch might help convince producers he was already a successful actor. So, she took him to a Beverly Hills jeweller, where he picked out a Rolex. The only problem? It cost him his entire savings. “I was eating spaghetti and boiled potatoes for months after that!” he recalled in a 2019 interview with the Fondation de la Haute Horlogerie.

It's touching anecdote given Stewart's modest upbringing. Having grown up in poverty, he admitted that it took him years to feel comfortable spending money on beautiful things. But eventually, he came to realize there was nothing wrong with enjoying the rewards of his success.

Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80 to the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix

One of Stewart's most entertaining watch moments came at the 2017 Canadian Grand Prix, when he joined Daniel Ricciardo on the podium and celebrated the Australian driver's third-place finish by drinking champagne from his racing boot. Yes, really.

And while most people were understandably distracted by the sight of Captain Picard downing champagne from a sweaty shoe, there was a rather lovely watch on his wrist, a Tissot Le Locle Powermatic 80. The reasonably priced Swiss automatic features a silver guilloché dial, Roman numerals, a black leather strap, and an impressive 80-hour power reserve. Proof that even a relatively affordable dress watch can look the part.

Long before his appearances at watchmaking galas, Stewart was already making a convincing case for the understated dress watch. In this archival portrait, the actor wears a small, gold-tone, rectangular timepiece, seemingly a Cartier Tank, on a dark leather strap. Paired with a pale linen-style suit and a knitted white top, it's a particularly handsome combination.

First introduced in 1917, the Cartier Tank has become one of watchmaking's most enduring designs, worn by everyone from Andy Warhol to Muhammad Ali. Its appeal lies in its simplicity: a rectangular case, elegant proportions, and a design that has barely needed changing in more than a century. And judging by this photograph, Stewart understood the appeal of a good dress watch long before his later affection for IWC became public knowledge.

IWC Portofino Automatic to the 2018 Academy Awards

When it came to dressing for the red carpet, Stewart understood the appeal of keeping things classic. At the 90th Academy Awards in 2018, he opted for an IWC Portofino Automatic, reference IW356504, a rather beautiful dress watch that proved you don't need an enormous, diamond-covered timepiece to make an impression. Housed in a 40mm case of 18-carat rose gold, the watch features a silver-plated dial, slim gold hour markers, and a discreet date window at three o'clock. It's finished with a dark brown alligator leather strap and powered by an automatic movement. There's nothing particularly flashy about it, which is precisely what makes it so appealing.

IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic at the 2017 SIHH Gala

By 2017, Stewart's appreciation for fine watchmaking had brought him into the company of some of the industry's most celebrated names. That January, he attended IWC Schaffhausen's Decoding the Beauty of Time gala in Geneva alongside his wife, Sunny Ozell, sporting a rather handsome IWC Portugieser Chronograph Classic.

First introduced in 2013, the reference 3904 is a particularly elegant piece of watchmaking. Featuring a 42mm case, distinctive Arabic numerals, and a railway-track minute scale, it's a slightly sportier proposition than the Portofino. Inside sits IWC's automatic calibre 89361, complete with a flyback chronograph function and an impressive 68-hour power reserve. The evening celebrated the Swiss manufacturer's renewed Da Vinci collection, but Stewart's choice of wristwear showcased another of IWC's most recognizable designs. And given that he once named IWC as his favorite watchmaker, it's hardly surprising that he had such good taste.

#6740. 7 Peter Millar Staples That Work Wherever the Day Takes You

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Good clothes, fewer decisions. What’s not to like?

Getting dressed for just about anything is a whole lot easier when you have the right essentials in your closet. Whether you’re heading into the office, catching a flight, or packing for a weekend away, a few well-chosen pieces can take you from one plan to the next without having to give your outfit much thought.

That’s the appeal of Peter Millar’s Crown Collection, which brings together classic menswear with thoughtful details, quality fabrics, and a relaxed approach to dressing well. There’s an emphasis on comfort and practicality, but never at the expense of looking polished. From tailored trousers and half-zips to quilted layers and other everyday favorites, these are pieces you'll find yourself reaching for again and again.

Ahead, seven picks from the collection, available at Bloomingdale’s, that deserve a spot in your closet. Because looking good shouldn’t require a whole lot of effort.

#6739. Designers Say THIS Is the Secret to Making Framed Posters Look Like Expensive Art

Your prized prints deserve better than a thumbtack.

Posters have come a long way since your dorm-room days. A vintage travel ad, a favorite movie poster, or a concert print you’ve kept for decades can bring as much character to a room as an original painting. The key to giving them a more polished look? A thoughtful frame, creative placement, and the confidence to display what you love.

"Never overthink putting posters up in your home, especially concert posters," says interior designer J.D. Bell. "Your home should reflect your personality, interests, and what you love."

Here, designers share their favorite ways to give posters the elevated treatment they deserve.

The quickest way to transform a poster into a statement piece is to ditch the inexpensive clip frame in favor of something more substantial. Consider rich wood, sleek metal, or even an ornate antique frame that contrasts with the poster's graphic quality.

"The key to making them feel elevated is to treat them with the same consideration you would a fine piece of art," says designer Hattie Sparks Collins. "A beautiful frame, thoughtful installation, and a little juxtaposition go a long way."

For particularly colorful or busy posters, a generous mat can provide visual breathing room, while a frame that picks up one of the artwork's less prominent colors creates a subtle, sophisticated connection.

Have a stack of concert posters gathering dust? Turn them into a gallery wall that tells your story.

Bell recently displayed a collection of vibrant Dead & Company concert posters against black-and-gray Zak + Fox patterned wallpaper in a client's home. Rather than using identical frames, he selected a mix of black styles to introduce texture while maintaining cohesion.

"The darker backdrop really made the colorful artwork pop," he explains. The collection was installed in a hallway leading to the client's office, a fitting tribute to his love of music.

The takeaway? Don't be afraid to experiment with patterned walls, unexpected locations, and subtly mismatched frames.

One of the most compelling ways to display posters is alongside furnishings that feel decidedly more traditional. Think a framed vintage movie poster above an antique chest, or a bold graphic print surrounded by rich textiles and classic upholstery.

"I really love pairing graphic, colorful posters with antiques, interesting textiles, and more traditional furnishings," Collins says. "That tension between high/low and old/new is what makes a room feel collected instead of decorated."

Ultimately, the most stylish poster is one that means something to you. Forget whether it's rare, valuable, or considered fine art. A beautifully framed memento from your favorite concert can become the most interesting piece in the room.

As Bell puts it, "What matters is making those pieces feel special and intentional. The right framing can do a lot for a poster and make it feel like a real piece of art."
