#6661. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#6655. One of My Least Favorite Politicians Lost an Election This Week. I Can’t Stop Laughing About It.

This guy was an ur-Trumper before Trump, and I’m so glad we’re rid of him.

This one is for me. Pardon me while I bust a gut.

Kris Kobach of Kansas—and never has a phrase with three K’s been more apt—is one of the founding weasels of the modern voter-suppression effort. If there is an odious policy position concocted since 2008, Kobach has run the laboratory that produced it. Imaginary voter fraud? Kobach led the farcical election-integrity commission during Trump I that fell apart after a contentious 2017 hearing in New Hampshire at which Kobach’s contentions were disemboweled by Maine secretary of state Matthew Dunlap. Draconian immigration policies? As Kansas secretary of state, Kobach tried to initiate a “proof of citizenship” law that lined him up on the wrong side of the League of Women Voters. Outright racism? Kobach told a radio program that the Obama administration was not far from a call for an end to all prosecutions of African Americans. If there was an ur-Trumper before Trump, it was Kris Kobach.

I thought we were rid of him when he ran for governor in 2018 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he ran for the Senate in 2020 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he got tangled up with Steve Bannon’s Build the Wall scam. But he was like the shingles virus in the body politic. He got elected Kansas attorney general in 2022.

And, on Tuesday, and extending into Thursday afternoon, it looks as though Kris Kobach might have lost another election. May it be his last.

#6654. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

#6651. Godzilla Minus Zero Is a Sequel Over 50 Years in the Making

The secret to the terrifying monster melee film? “I want the audience to feel that anxiety,” director Takashi Yamazaki tells Esquire.

However much Takashi Yamazaki loves Godzilla, he might love his rival more. In 2023, the filmmaker wowed the world with Godzilla Minus One, an emotionally stirring disaster epic that told a deeply human story about sacrifice, self-forgiveness, and the power of found families, against the backdrop of postwar Japan. At the 96th Academy Awards, Godzilla Minus One clinched the Oscar for Best Visual Effects, which not only righteously recognized its handsome design but lent credence to its rep as a prestige-level event film.

But many years ago, its director was just a seven-year-old boy eager to imagine his hometown wrecked by Ghidorah, one of Godzilla’s most prolific enemies. “In the theaters, fifty-five years ago, was the first time I saw Ghidorah,” Yamazaki tells Esquire via English interpreter. It was at a revival screening of the 1964 film Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster, when a school-aged Yamazaki saw a gold dragon with three heads stomp over Matsumoto City, where his family lived. But instead of glee, Yamazaki actually felt crushing ... disappointment.

“I was looking forward to seeing the utter destruction of my hometown by that kaiju,” Yamazaki says with a laugh. “They didn’t go as far as I wanted them to. So what I’ve done here is my childhood dream come true.”

On Friday, November 6, the King of the Monsters returns to the screen in Godzilla Minus Zero. Set in 1949, the sequel continues the story of Godzilla Minus One’s ex-kamikaze pilot Kōichi (Ryunosuke Kamiki). It’s been two years since Godzilla’s rise, and now he and his wife Noriko (Minami Hamabe) are raising their adopted daughter Akiko (Sae Nagatani). But when Ghidorah shows up, Kōichi regroups with his colleagues who’ve since formed the Large-Scale Disaster Response Bureau. While trying to stop Ghidorah’s terrible rampage, they cook up a desperate, if inevitable plan: Convince Godzilla to fight him.

Although Yamazaki spent the promotional cycle of Godzilla Minus One insisting that he had no plans for a sequel, the director had actually drummed up a story outline for Minus Zero. Yamazaki tells Esquire that he wanted to keep following the characters he introduced in his previous film to see how their traumatic circumstances could bring them closer together.

“In this series, Minus One and Minus Zero, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker says. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge. I wanted to see how they were going to deal with that. I love this family. I’m very attached to this family. I just wanted to see how they march forward.”

Below, Yamazaki reveals how surprised he was by the Western reception toward his first movie, why he included Ghidorah in the sequel, and whether a third movie is in the cards.

This interview has been edited for clarity, with Japanese-to-English translation performed by an interpreter.

ESQUIRE: Let’s rewind to the release of Godzilla Minus One in 2023. The movie was acclaimed outside Japan, particularly in the United States, while critics in Japan were less enthused. Did this response surprise you?

YAMAZAKI: The way Minus One was received in the United States was a surprise to me. We made Minus One without international audiences in mind. Step by step, it grew larger; first there was the L.A. premiere, which was received with a passionate reaction. That made me happy. Then the release came, and we started to see all of these comments and reaction clips on YouTube. That was surprising. I feel grateful because it was something I had not foreseen or imagined.

Why do you think there was such a stark difference towards Minus One from U.S. audiences versus Japan?

U.S. fans are so expressive. We tend to be more reserved. We don’t wear our excitement on our sleeves. I guess this is just how American people are versus Japanese. But to experience that kind of reception was wonderful. I remember watching American movies and imagining how great it would be to put a film out there and have that kind of reaction. That is what came true. So I’m very grateful.

When you first revealed Godzilla Minus Zero, it was with a teaser that showed Godzilla stomping into New York City. What compelled you to make this the very first look at the movie?

I wanted the first [look] of Minus Zero to be impactful. To have people wondering, “What the hell is going to happen?” Because the first film was so wonderfully and warmly received among American audiences, this was a way for me to communicate to the American audience, “Your country is part of the story.”

Between these two movies, you make the point that just because you aren’t related by blood or marriage doesn’t mean you can’t be a family. Where does this story come from? Do you have any similar relationships in your life?

This does not come from my personal experience, but it is something I touched on in a film I made about twenty years ago, Always: Sunset on Third Street. I feel that, if a parent and child are non-biological, sometimes their connection is stronger than biological parents and children. One of my inspirations is Shuhei Fujisawa. One of his novels is a story about a ronin who accepts a mother and child, albeit reluctantly at first, as his own family. I’ve been inspired and affected by that.

Godzilla Minus Zero continues the postwar period setting you introduced in the first film. What about Japan’s status in the immediate aftermath of World War II makes it fertile ground for the stories you want to tell with Godzilla?

With Godzilla Minus One, I attempted to tell the story of, What do people do when they are lost? When they are stripped of all means to fight? With this one, it’s a continuation of that story, about people who have to fight without anything to fight with when an even greater catastrophe than Minus One befalls them. I also wanted to tell the story of people not using weapons, but their brains. Their wits and wisdom. In this film, you see how they have to resort to means no human being should resort to. Trying to put an end to it all, but having to resort to taboo means.

Out of all of Godzilla’s other rivals, what excites you most about Ghidorah?

I was born in the Year of the Dragon, so it just so happens I love dragons. But it’s also the form factor of this kaiju. It straddles the line; if it were any weirder as a form, it would be really weird. But it’s cool visually. It’s a strange kaiju you can’t compare with anything. And it’s been Godzilla’s archnemesis for years, and the strongest nemesis of Godzilla. I don’t know how many more Godzilla films I will be invited to do, so I didn’t want to save Ghidorah for later.

Sometimes the spectacle of Godzilla fighting another monster undermines the dramatic weight he’s meant to carry. How did you strike the balance between maintaining Godzilla’s terrifying aura and the blockbuster thrills of watching him fight?

The way I approached this, in what we call a versus film, I wanted to portray how mankind relates to Godzilla and Ghidorah. Godzilla is a wild beast that humans have to deal with. Mankind harbors fear and awe in the face of Godzilla. But as I mentioned, they have to step into a taboo [solution], which mankind must never step into, to deal with this godlike beast. It is that fear and awe and conflict I wanted to depict. It was never my intention to have Godzilla as a friendly kaiju.

Godzilla has appeared in many different shades over the years. He’s been a monster, a folk hero, even a mascot. How do you, personally, see Godzilla? Is your depiction of him in your films reflective of how you think the monster should be?

I see Godzilla as a very angry god, or the wrath of God, because it is a metaphor for nuclear weapons and war. If we are going to tell a story set in the 1940s, after World War II, relying on Godzilla’s powers is tantamount to relying on nuclear weapons. Godzilla has that much of an immense power. There’s no way we can utilize his power without suffering repercussions. There is no happiness on the other side.

There have been a lot of Godzilla movies made in Hollywood lately. To your view, what are American filmmakers getting right or wrong?

I have no complaints about the different ways in which Godzilla is depicted. That’s actually one of the things I like about Godzilla. There’s room for avenues and perspectives. You also have Showa Era Godzilla that is quite funny. Then you have the Hollywood Godzilla, the monster battle films. They’re all wonderful. It’s great Godzilla can encompass all of these forms. I don’t intend at all to say, “This Godzilla is good” and “This Godzilla is not right.”

When Godzilla Minus One first released, there was so much talk about how it achieved so much with a limited budget. With Godzilla Minus Zero having more money than its predecessor, what were you able to accomplish on a technical level that wasn’t possible before?

We put all of that extra money into the visuals. I don’t want you looking at a movie, I want you in the story. There are a couple scenes where it’s longer takes, in which we show the kaiju coming and the people are scrambling, trying to escape but there’s nowhere to go. I want the audience to feel that anxiety. It is also the first Japanese film filmed for IMAX. So we were able to realize that immersive in-theater experience. It’s not a film, it’s an experience.

What are the chances of a black-and-white “Minus Color” version of Godzilla Minus Zero?

I don’t know. Personally, I want to make it. It’s of the times, isn’t it? It feels like a documentary. It’s something I want to do. I don’t know if I’ll do it yet.

You said during Minus One you didn’t plan on doing a sequel. So I’m asking now, after Minus Zero, do you have plans for a third movie?

It would be great if we could do a third film. But we have to have an offer before we embark on a new project. I’m also curious what other people might do with Godzilla in other films. Nothing is planned yet.

If there’s a third movie, do you imagine including other enemies from Godzilla’s history? I ask as a big Destroyah fan...

[Laughs]. I’ll refrain from any comments that might be misconstrued as huge news. But it would be interesting to have the option of a completely new kaiju appear. Already existing kaiju would be great, but new kaiju too.

You’re now working on your first English-language Hollywood movie, Grandgear. How are you adapting after a long career making movies in Japan?

It’s like I’m starting all over again. It makes me feel the way I felt when I was making my debut in Japan. I don’t really care about making films in Hollywood versus making films in Japan, as long as I can embark on a project that I truly want to make going forward. No matter the territory.

#6650. Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

#6649. Reviewing Ross Lynch’s Low-Key Relationship History Now That He’s a Married Man (!!)

Yes, his wife’s identity is still mostly a mystery.

If you missed the headlines this morning, Ross Lynch is now a married man…and if you’re thinking, “Um, I didn’t even know he was dating anyone,” you’re not alone. Despite hard-launching his relationship status at the Heartland premiere—telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married—the actor and musician has yet to even confirm his wife’s name, much less how they met or how long they’ve been together.

Luckily, I’ve done the work of piecing together Ross’s very low-key relationship history for you, from his Teen Beach Movie love triangle, to his six-year relationship with his Chilling Adventures of Sabrina costar, to his mysterious new wife (!!). Scroll ahead for everything I’ve uncovered.

Okay, it’s unclear if these two actually dated, but Ross did tell Cosmo (hi!) during an April 2019 interview that he was involved in “a love triangle” on the set of Disney’s Teen Beach Movie.

“I was 16 at the time of the first movie, but it was basically a bunch of high school students or college students,” he told Cosmo (via Seventeen). “And they just put us on an island in the Caribbean for two months.”

He never named who was involved in the love triangle, but this flirty Instagram pic ^^ that he posted with backup dancer Morgan Larson in July 2013 led fans to believe that something possibly went down between the two.

Again, this one was only ever rumored, but fans suspect that Ross’s Teen Beach Movie costar Maia was possibly the other person involved in the supposed “love triangle.” The reason? While chatting with Hollywood Life in June 2015, Ross shared that he and Maia had an unscripted kiss in the sequel film.

“Maia and I did a kiss spontaneously,” he said. “We thought that Mac and Brady should have an onscreen kiss. It wasn’t scripted and the director didn’t tell us to do it and we just did it and hopefully it makes it in the movie!” Oh, and he also added that he thought Maia was a good kisser. Moving on!

In September 2015, Ross became Instagram-official with Yellowjackets star Courtney after reportedly meeting on the set of the movie Status Update. The two went on to date for just about two years before going their separate ways, with Courtney being described as Ross’s “then-girlfriend” during a November 2017 interview with W.

A month later in December 2017, Ross and Courtney were spotted having lunch together, but it doesn’t seem like they ever officially reconciled.

Dating rumors about these two began shortly after Ross and Jaz met on the set of Chilling Adventures of Sabrina, but they really ramped up after Ross’s sister Rydel shared a Snapchat of Ross and Jaz kissing on New Year's Eve in 2018. However, when asked about their relationship by Entertainment Tonight later that month, Ross insisted that Jaz was just his “closest friend.” Mmkay.

Just a few months later in April 2019, Us Weekly caught the couple getting cozy in NYC, and at long last in January 2020, the costars finally made their red carpet debut together at the Balmain show in Paris.

The couple kept fans swooning for the next couple of years until June 2023, when a fan shared a video of Lynch discussing heartbreak during one of his live shows with his band The Driver Era, sparking breakup rumors. “The thing about heartbreak is the only thing that really helps is music,” he told the audience at the time.

Following his split from Jaz (and rumors of a brief relationship with a woman named Indie Wolper), Ross seemingly soft-launched a new romance on TikTok in September 2024, which sent fans into a frenzy.

A few months later, Ross hinted that there was someone new in his life during a January 2025 interview with radio show Smallzy’s Surgery. Talking about The Driver Era song “Same Old Story,” Ross said, “It’s like really recent—like, maybe, I’m talking like a month ago. It’s about this experience I’ve been having recently with this girl.”

Reddit sleuths got to work and ID’d the new mystery woman as Anna, claiming the two met in Portugal. By early 2026, fans took to Reddit to claim that—when asked about his ring at a meet-and-greet—Ross confirmed he’d been married since before Christmas 2025.

Then, after a nearly year-long hiatus from Instagram, Ross reappeared on social media in June 2026, with his first photo showing him wearing what appeared to be said wedding ring.

“Been a minute, what did I miss?” he captioned the post, and when a fan slid into his comments to write, “more like what did WE miss ? 🤨💍” Ross replied, “hahaha A LOT 🤫.”

Finally, Ross cleared things up at the premiere of his new movie Heartland in November 2026, telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married.

“I’m not trying to shy away from that by any means, but I am trying to, you know, my wife’s not very fond of celebrity,” he said of keeping his relationship on the down-low. “So I am trying to be sensitive towards that and respectful to her. But everything’s going well.”

#6648. Hunter Schafer’s Studded Lime-Green Hoodie Is a One-of-a-Kind Statement

#6647. 7 Desert Boots That Can Survive the Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Despite the name, these chukkas are built to be worn in all conditions.

Winter weather can get ugly. If it’s not wind and rain, it’s slushy sidewalks and unexpected snow flurries. You obviously need shoes that can handle a little precipitation, but the key phrase here is a little. You don’t want to stomp around in heavy winter boots, especially if we want to look nice. Dress shoes are a tough fit in winter, as are dress boots. Funny enough, between December and March, you know what our go-to footwear becomes? Desert boots. Technically chukka boots, but a few desert boots make the cut here.

But that’s something to note. The Esquire office does not use the term interchangeably—but the Internet does, so we’re going along with it. We know some of you—nerds, mostly—have stopped reading this article because you don’t think we know the difference between the two. We’re well aware of the square and rectangle-ism: All desert boots are chukkas, not all chukkas are desert boots.

If you’re not a nerd and want to learn something new, chukkas are ankle-high boots with open lacing and two or three pairs of eyelets, often made with leather uppers—leather or rubber soled. Desert boots are a subsection of this silhouette that typically features a suede upper (nubuck, sometimes) with lighter-colored, more flexible crepe soles. They are not as winterized—suede, especially, is known to take a beating from moisture—hence the name. The details vary here and there, but both styles can work for winter if you choose wisely.

We found seven pairs of chukka boots—yes, some of which are desert boots—with weather-friendly features, from water-resistant uppers to sturdy rubber soles. Most have both, so you can find a pair that looks sharp without leaving you entirely at the mercy of the winter weather forecast.

These chukka boots might as well be dress boots. They look sharp no matter what outfit you pair them with, and the ultra-refined, premium calfskin leather upper is naturally water-resistant; to a point, that is—we wouldn’t wear them in the pouring rain.

They’re very comfortable thanks to a soft, leather lining on the inside and open-cell foam insoles that provide supportive cushioning. And the flexible outsoles made from XL Extralight injection molding are a nice touch that give the boot some lightweight flexibility.

For a classic pair of chukka boots that come at a good price and have a wide range of colors and sizes, Thursday Boot Co. is the way to go. These chukkas come in a variety of colors and materials, but our favorite has to be the Mocha colorway in weather-safe suede. It’s rare to find a pair of suede shoes you can actually get wet without ruining, but these ones can brave the weather while still looking great. And with studded rubber outsoles, you have some extra traction and durability. If you want even more traction and durability in the soles, try one of the Rugged & Resilient styles with anti-slip wedge rubber outsoles.

Here’s another suede option you can actually wear during the winter; Sabah treats the suede upper of this boot to be water resistant, but you can still add a suede treatment for extra protection if you want to. These boots are handmade in Monte San Giusto, Italy, and you can trust that they were made with lots of skill and care. The insole and midsole are made from vegetable tanned leather, so they end up forming to your feet once you wear them, making them extra comfortable.

If you like the look of a gum-sole sneaker, you might like how it looks on a boot, too. With these desert boots, SeaVees blends the classic gum-sole look with the more rugged desert boot style, and the result is a versatile boot that can withstand pretty much any weather. With water-resistant rugged oiled leather uppers and natural rubber soles, these guys can get you through quite a lot.

These are going to be the least weather-ready on this list. You don’t want to take them out in a snowstorm, but damn do they look good. Imagine these worn to a holiday party, worn with a nice pair of chinos and fair-isle sweater.

The suede upper is a true not-water-resistant suede. But they do have durable rubber soles. The stitched moc-toe is a fun detail that makes these chukka boots stand out from the rest of the pack. Drake’s notes that these boots run small, so they recommend sizing up by a half-size when ordering online.

We’re starting to get pretty far away from the chukka heart, but you can see the chukka-shaped vamp and the ankle-y shape. Plus, when we think of rugged workwear boots, we think of Timberland. The upper is made from waterproof leather and has a TimberDry waterproof membrane for extra protection, and you can see that Gripstick rubber outsole gives you some distance from the ground and provides great traction as well.

And finally, we can’t talk about chukka boots without mentioning Clarks. The original Clarks desert boot was designed in 1949 and inspired by the rough suede and crepe-soled boots worn by off-duty British soldiers during WWII (as well as the shoes worn by polo players in India and South African veldskoen shoes). Today, the brand’s original style is still going strong, and you can get a pair for under $100.

#6644. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6643. The Apple Watch Deals You Need to Look for This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We’re expecting record-low deals in the coming weeks—but for now, we’ll take $250 off.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on sale like we just did.

Apple Watches usually go on deepest discount during Amazon sales, Black Friday included. And while only two models are marked down right now (the Ultra 4 and Ultra 3), we know a thing or two about how Apple deals work. In the coming weeks, prices will drop and these wearables will sell out like hotcakes, which is why you’ll want to plan what you’re going to buy now.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set come Black Friday. If you don’t take the plunge on one before then, that is.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made all the sweeter when it inevitably goes back on sale for Black Friday.

For October Prime Day, this brand-new watch surprisingly dropped to just $350, so we’re expecting a similar—if not deeper—discount in the coming weeks.

While the Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t necessarily reinventing the wheel, it did get the same advanced health tracking and readiness score features from the Apple Watch Series 12, so if you’re looking to get your first Ultra model, it’s worth grabbing—especially when it’s $100 off. Not to mention, Guillory is partial to the new ocean-inspired, semi-translucent rubber watch bands.

That said, if you’re not someone who needs the shiny new gadget immediately or who won’t take advantage of these souped-up health features—you’re not going to miss out on much if you score an older model on sale, like this $250 discount on the Apple Watch Ultra 3. Associate Commerce Editor Bryn Gelbart went so far as to dub it the “best Apple Watch ever” when it was released last fall.

“I was never tempted to upgrade to the premium option, but the Apple Watch Ultra 3 changed my mind,” Gelbart said. “It turns out I didn't quite know what I was missing.” It has a bright 49mm display, satellite messaging features, a durable titanium case, and a long lasting battery (an impressive 42 hours under normal use and 72 hours on Low Power Mode). Gelbart recommends it for cyclists, runners, or the adventure-prone.

At 28% off, this is the best pre-Black Friday deal to shop as of publication, although this model has dipped as low as $600 before.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This dropped to a record-low $199 during October Prime Day, so we’re hopeful that a similar price cut will emerge for Black Friday, but this is by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market, even at full price.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, it’s worth grabbing quickly. During Prime Day, a handful of watches sold out completely—and you won’t want your wearable of choice to meet the same fate before you check out.

#6638. These 9 Brands Are Making the Best Dark-Wash Jeans on the Market

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Moody, rich, and sophisticated—everything you want to project with your dressing.

In the world of denim, think of dark wash jeans like a good Scotch. It exists within a broader umbrella, but it’s a little more weathered and more mature. In the grand scheme of jeans, you really can’t go wrong when it comes to different washes. For clarity, we use dark wash to describe lightly washed dark jeans, raw sanforized jeans, and the real nerdy loomstate shrink-to-fit stuff. Why? Because they are all, visually, dark wash. For us, it’s exactly how you should buy your jeans.

Jeans inherently skew casual. But the subdued color of dark wash allows it to pass for something a little more refined. Where other denim gets processed and faded after being dyed, dark wash hasn’t—letting it keep that subdued inky hue that veers slightly toward “polished trouser” territory. They’re more rugged than a pair of slacks, but just formal and sophisticated enough that you can probably wear them around the office as a sort of nonchalantly edgy work pant.

You won’t need to hunt far or hard for quality and stylish dark washes. Most, if not all, of the Esquire-editor-approved jeans and denim brands also offer darker colors—making it as easy as buying your favorite sweater in another color. Find your favorite below.

Rag & Bone knows what it’s good at and sticks to it. (It’s denim...they’re good at denim.) Our reviewers especially like how the brand approaches its sizing and fits. It’s a super simple and straightforward number system that starts at 1 and goes up to 4. Fit 1 offers a slimmer silhouette, while Fit 4 gives that classic straight look.

For their dark wash—which Rag & Bone calls “Solomon”—the brand uses its very own rb Selvedge, which it developed in partnership with one of Japan's premier denim mills. This will help the jeans naturally develop a nice patina over time to really show just how much you love your denim.

If you ask Esquire shopping editor Luke Guillory what jeans to buy, he’s going to tell you these. He’s already waxed poetic about Wrangler’s original Cowboy Cut in our denim buying guide, so we’ll just cut to the chase: they’re super flattering and at an ultra-competitive price (though he advises bigger guys should opt for the more relaxed cut).

If you’re looking for something under $100, Gap’s more than likely where it’s at. The retailer has spent a ton of time and effort in reupping its basics—jeans especially. The brand’s classic straight silhouette is, at this point in time, better than the offering from the big-name brand that starts with “L” and rhymes with WiFi.

People hunt for vintage Levi’s for a reason. These 505s are a reproduction of the brand’s iconic style for 1967. As Luke describes in our denim buying guide, the fit will “hug up top, drape down low.” “They're universally flattering and classic cut, so you don't have to worry about them going out of style,” he says. “As long as they're holding on, you'll look good.”

We’ve already rounded up a few Todd Snyder pieces we’d recommend buying, and these dark indigo worn wash jeans are one more thing to add to the list. Available in three different fits—slim, straight, and relaxed—it’s made from an all-cotton denim that balances comfort and durability.

This denim from Ralph Lauren’s Americana sublabel is an absolute classic. It’s got a distinctly Western feel with its deep 13.75 oz selvedge denim, while still offering that sort of rugged sophistication you want from a dark wash jean.

Little known fact, but Acne Studios actually started out with jeans. Over 20 years later, it’s still their bread and butter—with funky pieces that embody a “rock and roll edge,” as Luke describes them. This groovy wide-legged pair especially gives off a sort of “Lenny-Kravitz-meets-Steven-Tyler” air of vintage swagger.

We’re long-time fans of Buck Mason. As Esquire contributor Matthew Medendrop describes, the brand has been “quietly doing some of the most exciting things in contemporary menswear” for the last few years—and their denim is no exception. The brand’s “full saddle” jeans take inspiration from the classic vintage blue jeans, think Levi’s 501s circa like the ‘60s. Pair that with the deep dark wash, and you got one sexy pair of jeans.

3Sixteen is one of the premier brands for Esquire’s denim nerds. It gets into the nitty-gritty stuff we care about, but since it’s based here in North America—and not in Japan—it’s a lot easier to get your hands on. To break down the naming, RS means relaxed straight, what we think is the easiest to wear from the brand. The 100xk tag is the type of denim—custom woven for the brand by Kabuki Mills. It’s rough at first, be warned, but it softens and fades like no other.

#6635. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: ARBO Landscape Architects Location: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dov on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6631. The Denim Trends You’ll Be Seeing All Winter Long

These four styles are about to dominate the street style on your FYP.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Let’s talk denim. You probably already have a solid rotation of jeans in your closet (and if you don’t, what in the world have you been wearing this fall?), but it never hurts to add to it. And you’re going to want to add to it, especially because this winter’s styles are going to be so good.

We’re not just talking low-rise or bootcut; we’re getting specific with it. Don’t worry, there’s a trend out there for everyone. If you’re feeling bold, you might find yourself taking an interest in the animal-print barrel-leg styles (Mango’s Bambi jeans have been ever-popular this season) that we saw striding into runway shows this fall. Or, perhaps, Chloé’s ultra-pleated denim has the dramatic flair that you’ve been coveting. On the other hand, if you’re looking for a subtle style to bring into your wardrobe folds, an inconspicuously ripped pair or a baggy low-rise cut might be the newness you needed.

We’ve found the four denim trends set to take over this winter, so get ahead of the game and start shopping for a pair today. With a novelty jacket and a square-toe boot, your trend-forward outfit will be complete.

We’re not talking full 2000s shredded jeans; rather, we’re seeing a slight distressing that adds a casual worn-in feel to a new pair. In bootcut, straight, relaxed, and wide-leg silhouettes, this subtle rip is taking over the streets this season.

Both low-rise and baggy denim aren’t anything new, but we’re seeing both trends combined this winter, creating a slouchy, nonchalant vibe. We would recommend pairing them with a cropped puffer or fitted jacket so that the bagginess doesn’t overwhelm your figure.

Chloé put an avant-garde spin on pleated pants, going for a drop-waist denim with a voluminous leg. We’re digging the exaggerated pleats, and we’ve found similarly dramatic designs across price points (in case the $2,200 price tag isn’t speaking to you).

Take a ride on the wild ride with these animal-print barrel-leg pairs. We’ve been loving the prints—cheetah, zebra, cow, leopard, and so on—and the distinct barrel silhouette, so naturally, blending the two trends together is really working for us.

#6628. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

Inside a Highly Functional Georgetown Row House
